--- Page 1 ---
<meta>This page concludes the theological dialogue between a Lutheran and a person previously identified as a "Papist" (a Catholic friar), resulting in the latter's conversion and their joint decision to flee to Lutheran territories.</meta>

Now, further: 1. I will also show you that we are just as certain of our <term>salvation<note>The original German "Seligkeit" refers to the state of being blessed or saved in eternity.</note></term> as the Apostle Paul was.

In the Bible, to be sure, not all people are listed by name, even though they are all meant by it.

The Bible does not say: Pope Clement, Urbanus, Luther, Ignatius, or Calvin shall be saved; and yet, it is not possible that at least one of these should not have been saved.

But here is what is written in Mark 16, verse 16: "Whoever believes and is baptized shall be saved." I and you both believe, and we were baptized in the name of the Holy Trinity (Matthew 28, verse 20). Therefore, we shall be saved. "Whoever turns from evil and does good shall have a good life and good days" (Psalm 34, verses 12–13). We both intend, through divine assistance, to turn from evil and do good; therefore we shall have a good life and good days—though hardly in this world, then certainly in the next. Ezekiel 18, verses 20 and 32: "Whoever turns from their transgression shall not die because of their iniquity, but shall live." We both now turn from our transgression, therefore we shall not die, but live. For, because that same passage speaks of the eternal death of the unrepentant, it likewise speaks of the eternal life of the repentant, and does not deal with temporal life (which both types of people possess). God wills that all people be helped; now, we are also people, therefore He also wills that we be helped. God has consigned all things under sin, so that He might have mercy on all (Romans 11). We, too, have hitherto been consigned under sin; therefore He wills to have mercy on us as well. As surely as He is a living God, He does not desire the death of the sinner; I and you are also sinners, therefore He does not desire our death either, etc.

->**The Catholic**<-

O, what more comforting conclusion could be reached! My final declaration and advice is that we immediately renounce the Papist errors, go to Lutheran places to join their preachers, follow their advice, look to the counsel of our souls, and obtain and keep a gracious God. That is my only wish, and my only desire.

->**The Lutheran**<-

I could wish for nothing dearer or more blessed right now. Therefore, I gladly follow your suggestion; let us set out on our journey tomorrow, discuss God’s Word further with one another, and make it known to all the world that out of me, an apostate, has come a renewed Lutheran, and out of you, a Papist <term>Friar<note>Original: "OrdensBruder," literally a brother of a religious order.</note></term>, a converted Evangelical Christian; let happen to us what will.

| | |
|---|---|
| We will gladly do without | Through your harsh, bitter death, |
| temporal honor; | We pray this of you, O Lord and God; |
| May you (O Christ) only | Nothing more: for my dear Lord, |
| grant us the Eternal, | Let your death be my life; |
| Which you have earned. | You have paid the price for me. |

->Go forth, men, and preserve yourselves for these favorable things.<note>original: "Ite VIrI hIs VosMet rebVs serVate seCVnDIs." This is a chronogram based on Virgil’s Aeneid. The capitalized letters (V+I+V+I+I+V+M+V+V+C+V+D+I) function as Roman numerals (5+1+5+1+1+5+1000+5+5+100+5+500+1) which sum to the year 1634.</note><-

->**T H E   E N D**<-

<summary>This concluding page uses logical syllogisms based on scripture to assure the characters of their salvation, ending with the Catholic friar's total conversion to Lutheranism. The two resolve to flee together to Protestant lands, renouncing worldly status for spiritual certainty.</summary>
<keywords>salvation, conversion, syllogism, baptism, Lutheranism, Papist, chronogram, 1634, religious exile</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition from the previous theological dialogue. While the preceding text concluded a religious debate between a Lutheran and a convert, this document is a formal title page for a political and military declaration issued by John George I, Elector of Saxony, during the height of the Thirty Years' War in 1631.</meta>

# COPY
# OF THE RESOLUTION,

->Which<-

# His Electoral Highness of Saxony, etc.
->gave to the Royal Spanish sub-delegated Envoy / on the 19th of October, **the Year 1631.**<-
<note>original: "Copia Resolutionis"</note>

->Along with the related<-

# Appendices /

From which everyone may perceive how very faithfully and diligently His Highness has at all times been concerned so that the manifold grievances, in which the obedient and faithful <term>Estates of the Empire<note>The 'Reichsstände' were the various princes, prelates, counts, and cities that held a seat and a vote in the Imperial Diet of the Holy Roman Empire.</note></term> are involved, might be remedied; the laws of the Empire and <term>German Liberty<note>A political term used by German princes to describe their traditional rights and autonomy against the perceived overreach or "absolute" power of the Holy Roman Emperor.</note></term> preserved; all extreme measures prevented; while on the other hand, amicable treaties promoted; and also good trust and a lasting, secure peace might be restored.

As well as how His Electoral Highness justified the <term>Leipzig Resolution<note>The 'Leipziger Schluß' of 1631 was a declaration by Protestant princes to form a defensive union and demand the repeal of the Edict of Restitution, which had stripped Protestants of their land.</note></term> with firm foundation and permanence, and why He also stood firm in it.

But how innocently and cruelly His Electoral Highness, as well as His lands and people, were persecuted because of it, and were finally forced and compelled to the utmost to join with the army of His Royal Majesty in Sweden, etc., to give open battle to the <term>General Count von Tilly<note>Johann Tserclaes, Count of Tilly, was the commander of the Catholic League’s forces. He had recently sacked the city of Magdeburg, an event that horrified the Protestant world.</note></term>, and to protect Himself and His faithful subjects.

---

->After the Birth of **CHRIST**<-
->In the Year **1631.**<-

<summary>This title page introduces a formal resolution by the Elector of Saxony explaining his forced alliance with Gustavus Adolphus of Sweden against the Imperial forces in 1631. It serves as a political justification for the Elector's shift from attempted neutrality to active military resistance during the Thirty Years' War.</summary>
<keywords>John George I of Saxony, Thirty Years' War, Leipzig Resolution, German Liberty, Count von Tilly, Gustavus Adolphus, Spanish diplomacy, 1631</keywords>

# List
## Of the Appendices belonging to
## this Resolution.

->No. 1.<-

Memorandum <note>original: "Memorial"</note> / which the envoys of His Electoral Highness of Saxony, etc., delivered to His Roman Imperial Majesty in the year 1630 at Vienna.

->No. 2.<-

Copy <note>original: "Copia"</note> of the letter from His Electoral Highness of Saxony, etc., to His Imperial Majesty, dated August 24, 1630.

->No. 3.<-

Copy <note>original: "Copia"</note> of the letter from the Electors, Princes, and <term>Estates<note>The 'Estates' (Stände) were the various political entities—such as princes, counts, and imperial cities—that held a seat in the Imperial Diet.</note></term> present at the <term>Convention of Leipzig<note>A significant assembly held in early 1631 where Protestant leaders attempted to form a neutral bloc and demand the repeal of the Edict of Restitution.</note></term>, as well as from the councilors and envoys of those absent, to His Roman Imperial Majesty, dated Leipzig, March 18, 1631.

->No. 4.<-

Copy of the Resolution <note>original: "Copia Resolutionis"</note>, which His Electoral Highness of Saxony, etc., gave to the Imperial Envoy on May 20, 1631.

->No. 5.<-

Copy <note>original: "Copia"</note> of the personal letter <note>original: "Handschreibens"</note> from His Electoral Highness of Saxony, etc., to His Imperial Majesty on May 20, 1631.

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page follows a list of diplomatic correspondence from 1631. It transitions from a summary of letters to a formal account of a specific diplomatic mission from the Spanish crown to the Elector of Saxony during a pivotal moment of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut headpiece at the top of the page features a symmetrical decorative design. It depicts reclining human figures, possibly nymphs or putti, integrated into a dense arrangement of scrolling vines, leaves, and flowers.</image-desc>

What was—in the name and on behalf of the Most Illustrious and Most Mighty Prince and Lord, Lord Philip IV <note>Philip IV (1605–1665) was the King of Spain during the height of the Thirty Years' War, a member of the House of Habsburg.</note>, King in Spain, the Two Sicilies, Portugal, and the Algarves, Archduke of Austria, Duke of Burgundy, Brabant, and Milan, Count of Habsburg, Flanders, and Tyrol; by His Royal Majesty's highly esteemed embassy residing in Vienna, specifically His Excellency the Marquess of Cadreita <note>Lope Díez de Aux y Armendáriz, Marquess of Cadreita, was a prominent Spanish diplomat and naval commander.</note>, through his sub-delegate <term>sub-delegate: a person appointed to act as a representative on behalf of a higher official</term>, the well-born Lord Heinrich Paradys, Baron of Erschaide, appointed Colonel to His Roman Imperial Majesty and the aforementioned Royal Majesty—orally presented and proposed in a granted personal audience to the Most Illustrious and High-born Prince and Lord, Lord John George <note>John George I (1585–1656), the Elector of Saxony, whose shifting alliances were central to the conflict in Germany.</note>, Duke of Saxony, Jülich, Cleves, and Berg, Arch-Marshal and Prince-Elector of the Holy Roman Empire, Landgrave in Thuringia, Marquess of Meissen, Burgrave of Magdeburg, Count of the Mark and Ravensberg, Lord of Ravenstein; and what was thereafter submitted through a written memorandum <note>original: "Memorial"</note> and further expressed in verbal conference:

His Electoral Highness has understood this in greater detail from the contents, has maturely considered all sides, and has found it to be chiefly directed toward how often and many times His Royal Majesty has endeavored to [achieve] a wholesome <note>catchword: "heilſamen/" (wholesome or salutary). This likely refers to a "wholesome peace" or settlement within the Empire.</note>

<summary>This page introduces a formal diplomatic report regarding a mission from King Philip IV of Spain to Elector John George I of Saxony, delivered via the Spanish embassy in Vienna to discuss peace efforts within the Holy Roman Empire.</summary>

<keywords>Philip IV of Spain, John George I of Saxony, Marquess of Cadreita, Heinrich Paradys, diplomatic audience, Holy Roman Empire, Thirty Years' War, Spanish-Saxon relations, 1631</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal diplomatic proposal delivered by Heinrich Paradys on behalf of King Philip IV of Spain to John George I, Elector of Saxony. It follows the introductory titles and salutations from the previous page, moving into the core of the Spanish peace initiative during the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

...to achieve and maintain a wholesome and lasting peace. Especially, however, the Lord *Ambassadors* were commanded to take great care that the old love and friendship—which has always been maintained between His Royal Majesty and his most honored House of Austria <note>The Habsburg dynasty, which ruled both Spain and the Holy Roman Empire.</note> and His Electoral Grace—is still steadily *preserved*. 

And because, beyond all expectation, great *alterations* <note>original: "Alterationes" — referring to the shifting political and military alliances in the 1630s</note> have appeared, from which a separation of the old friendship is to be feared rather than the preservation of the old intimacy, love, peace, and unity; therefore His Royal Majesty's *intentions*, opinions, and thoughts are directed solely toward this: to do his utmost so that through his *interposition* <term>interposition: diplomatic mediation or intervention by a third party to settle a dispute</term> this misfortune might be *remedied*, provided that such a thing were acceptable to His Electoral Grace. 

For His Royal Majesty desired nothing more than the prosperous advancement of His Electoral Grace and his entire *posterity* <note>future generations and heirs</note>, as well as that of his laudable Electoral House, just as with his ancestors; he likewise desired to show him all love, loyalty, and friendship. For this reason, His Royal Majesty deemed it advisable that His Electoral Grace should disclose his *grievances* <note>original: "Gravamina" — formal legal and political complaints</note> and *claims* <note>original: "pratensiones" — demands for rights or territories</note> confidentially in writing, and thereafter *deputize* <note>appoint as representatives</note> two *qualified* persons to a certain location. 

At that point, the aforementioned embassy would see to it that His Roman Imperial Majesty—who is also King of Hungary and Bohemia—likewise appointed two suitable persons for this purpose. Whereupon the named Lord *Ambassador*, as an *intermediary* <note>original: "Interponent"</note> in the name of His Royal Majesty, intended to apply all diligence so that all *differences*, which...

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific Spanish proposal delivered by the sub-delegate Heinrich Paradys to the formal diplomatic response from John George I, Elector of Saxony. The Elector acknowledges the proposal for a separate peace between the houses of Saxony and Austria, distinguishing it from a broader "universal peace" involving all warring parties in the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

...might occur, they could be brought to a reconciliation in the best way; all of which, however, was intended solely for the aforementioned **Archducal House of Austria** <note>The Austrian branch of the Habsburgs, who held the Imperial throne.</note> and the **Electoral House of Saxony**, and not for a **universal peace** <term>universal-Frieden: a general peace treaty involving all participants of the Thirty Years' War, rather than a separate peace between individual states.</term>.

Upon this, **His Electoral Grace** <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony.</note> initially offers his service-minded thanks to the most highly esteemed **His Royal Majesty** <note>Philip IV, King of Spain.</note> for the Royal greeting offered, and in return conveys to His Royal Majesty his friendly and most willing services, friendship, and whatever more love and good His Electoral Grace is capable of. Should His Royal Majesty continue to fare happily and well, His Electoral Grace would hear of it with great joy; wishing to **GOD** that His Almighty Power will sustain and preserve His Royal Majesty in good and constant physical health and all Royal prosperity for a long time. 

Likewise, the Lord **Sub-delegate** <note>Heinrich Paradys, the diplomatic messenger.</note> will know to deliver the enclosed letter of response from His Electoral Grace to the highly distinguished **Spanish Ambassador**, along with the report of his friendship and goodwill, as well as wishes for all well-being.

Concerning the **main petition**, His Electoral Grace must rightly and highly praise that His Royal Majesty has taken it so much to heart to help regain a lasting peace for the distressed **Holy Roman Empire**, and also makes such friendly and good offers to His Electoral Grace and his entire highly laudable **Electoral House**; they wish also to [return] such for your...

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal response of John George I, Elector of Saxony, to the Spanish diplomatic overtures. The Elector emphasizes the long-standing alliance between the House of Saxony and the House of Austria (the Habsburgs) and asserts his lifelong commitment to the peace of the Holy Roman Empire.</meta>

...sty <note>Completing "Majesty" from the previous page.</note> [intends] to oblige **His Electoral Grace** <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony.</note> with all friendship and possible good services; in turn, [His Electoral Grace] has made this his highest priority, while at the same time dutifully requesting that **His Royal Majesty** <note>Philip IV of Spain.</note> might once again continue <note>original: "continuiren"</note> in his most laudable Christian intention <note>original: "intention"</note>.

**His Electoral Grace** also remembers here, quite well and deliberately, the great and constant loyalty, love, and friendship which has been honorably maintained for many countless years between the most laudable **Archducal House of Austria** <note>The Austrian Habsburgs.</note> and his own **Electoral House of Saxony**; what benefit, honor, and praise has thereby accrued to both most highly honored houses—indeed, how much good and prosperity has grown for the entire **Holy Roman Empire** <term>Holy Roman Empire: A complex political entity in Central Europe; during this period (1632), it was devastated by the internal and external conflicts of the Thirty Years' War.</term> because of it. Accordingly, since the time of his Electoral reign—attained through the grace of **GOD** and now in its twenty-first year <note>John George I became Elector in 1611; this places the text around 1632.</note>—**His Electoral Grace** has certainly allowed nothing to be of higher or greater concern to him than how such a laudable and useful trust might be well preserved <note>original: "conserviret"</note>, increased and strengthened more and more, and happily transferred <note>original: "transferiret"</note> to worthy posterity <note>original: "Posteritet"</note>; and all of this has been proven many times over, not only with words but with a faithful and upright heart in deed.

All of **His Electoral Grace’s** counsels, actions, cares, diligence, and efforts have at all times been solely and uniquely directed <note>original: "dirigiret"</note> and aimed toward how peace and tranquility—which even the wise pagans <note>A common rhetorical reference to classical Greek and Roman philosophers like Cicero, who valued civic peace.</note> regarded as the greatest good bestowed upon mankind—[might be established] in the Holy Roman...

<summary>Elector John George I of Saxony reaffirms his historic alliance with the House of Austria and asserts that his 21-year reign has been dedicated to preserving this bond and seeking peace for the Holy Roman Empire.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, completing the word "Roman" (Römischen) to describe the preservation of the Empire. It outlines the Elector's efforts to maintain peace, imperial authority, and the legal framework of the Holy Roman Empire.</meta>

[the Ro]man Empire maintained <note>Completing "Römischen" from the previous page.</note>; all emerging differences <note>original: "differentien"</note> always stilled immediately in the bud <note>original: "in herba," literally "in the blade [of grass]"</note>; that highly harmful mistrust—which is the plague of the state <note>original: "pestis Reip." (pestis Reipublicae)</note> and which also finally destroys the mightiest empires—cleared out of the way in time; and in its place, good trust established once again among the Estates <term>Estates: The "Stände" or constituent members of the Holy Roman Empire, including princes, counts, and imperial cities.</term>. But first and foremost, [His Electoral Grace has] shown all due loyalty, love, and obedience to the **Roman Imperial Majesty** as his most honored supreme head; preserved <note>original: "conserviret"</note> his proper authority <note>original: "Autoritet"</note>, without which no power can exist, as well as the immovable, highly-sworn fundamental laws <note>original: "fundamental Geseze"</note> and other wholesome Imperial Constitutions <note>original: "Reichs Constitutiones"</note> by which the Head and the Members <note>A common metaphor where the Emperor is the "Head" and the Estates are the "Members" of the imperial body.</note> are bound, and which are the true **adamantine bonds of peace and tranquility** <note>original: "Adamantina pacis & tranquillitatis vincula"</note>; protected the Electors <note>original: "Chur-Fürsten"</note> and Estates in their privileges; and left everyone in Law and Justice, as the proven foundations and firmaments <note>original: "firmamentis"</note> of happy and stable government laid by Almighty **GOTT** Himself, not burdened against them; and also [ensured] that the **German Liberty** <term>German Liberty: A legal concept referring to the rights and autonomy of the individual German princes and states against the absolute power of the Emperor.</term>—acquired <note>original: "acquirirete"</note> so dearly through the shedding of so much brave and noble blood, and at all times maintained <note>original: "mantenirte"</note> with heroic bravery through Divine Grace—might not be oppressed.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What most humble loyalty, love, obedience, and constancy **His Electoral Highness** has at all times shown to the **Roman Imperial Majesty**; what loyal, useful, brave, and memorable services [he has rendered] to the same, as well as to the entire most laudable **House of Austria**, when significant adversities <note>original: "Adversiten"</note> and misfortunes threatened to meet and strike them—and indeed at a time when, to human [perception]...

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the Elector's personal sacrifices for the Empire. The previous page ended with "menschli-", which is completed here as "menschlichen" (human).</meta>

...[human] eyes <note>Completing "menschlichen" from the previous page.</note> certainly stood in a very precarious and dangerous state; and true loyalty and love were clearly recognizable. **His Electoral Serenity**, with undaunted risk to his electoral body and life, highness and dignity, as well as land and people, performed what services he could. What great danger, hatred, and ill-will from many noble houses and persons **His Electoral Serenity** has thereby drawn upon himself and his House and "loaded upon his neck" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning to take on a heavy or troublesome burden.</note>—all this is not only known throughout the Empire, but is notorious <note>original: "notori"</note> and manifest to all of **Christendom** and neighboring peoples. Therefore, His Electoral Serenity deems it entirely unnecessary to touch upon this with more words at this time.

In this due obedience and devotion <note>original: "Devotion"</note> toward Your **Imperial Majesty**, as well as love and affection toward the Archducal House of **Austria**, **His Electoral Serenity** has also remained firm, constant, upright, and unchanged at all times. In turn, he has most humbly and kindly expected and felt entirely assured of nothing other than Imperial just rule, favor, and grace, as well as protection and defense from Your **Imperial Majesty**—as a just, most kind, and mild Emperor—as well as friendship and goodwill from the most laudable Archducal House of **Austria**. This is especially so because even the rational heathens <note>A reference to ancient Greek and Roman philosophers and rulers who, despite not being Christian, were admired for their civic virtues.</note> rewarded such constant loyalty and useful services with good; and significant promises were made to **His Electoral Serenity** that his actions should likewise be well remunerated <note>original: "remunerirt"; meaning rewarded or compensated</note> and not forgotten, for both himself and his entire House.

<summary>The Elector recounts the great personal and political risks he took to serve the Emperor, noting that his loyalty is famous throughout Christendom. He asserts his continued devotion to the House of Austria, expecting that his services will be justly rewarded as promised.</summary>

<keywords>loyalty, House of Austria, Imperial Majesty, remuneration, risks of office, Christendom, Holy Roman Empire</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the Elector's defense of his conduct. While the previous page focused on his personal loyalty and sacrifices, this page addresses potential accusations of impropriety and describes the devastating impact of the ongoing war on the Empire’s legal and social order.</meta>

**His Electoral Serenity** is also not at all aware that he has permitted the slightest thing to occur which could rightfully be presented to the aforementioned **His Imperial Majesty** as unpleasant or improper, or that could be interpreted as inadmissible according to **Imperial laws** and traditions, or that could have been reasonably taken ill by the most laudable Archducal House of **Austria**.

It cannot be denied, however, that **His Electoral Serenity**—after great tribulation occurred within the **Holy Roman Empire** due to the unheard-of hardships of war, the incessant, numberless, and measureless recruitments, <note>original: "Marchen vnd Remarchen"</note> marches and counter-marches, plundering, quartering of troops, violent and heavy <term>contributions</term><note>Compulsory payments of money or supplies extorted from a population by occupying or passing military forces.</note>, and other innumerable enormities and excesses—has sent various most humble, obedient, and moving letters to **His Imperial Majesty** over several years. These hardships have desolated, devastated, and ruined one land and city after another. Furthermore, all respect for the **Electors**, Princes, and Estates has been entirely cast aside by the undisciplined <term>soldiery</term><note>The "soldatesca," referring to the mercenary or regular troops whose behavior during the Thirty Years' War often lacked any legal or moral restraint.</note>, who have insulted and burdened them in manifold ways, despised the **Imperial laws**, destroyed all good order, and in short, behaved in the lands of the **Electors** and Estates as if these territories had already been reduced to servitude. In his letters, he set forth with considerable detail what misery, distress, lamentation, and damage could grow from such calamity—not only for the **Holy Roman Empire** but also for **His Imperial Majesty’s** own most glorious Archducal House—most humbly and faithfully <note>Catchword: "trewlich" (faithfully). The sentence continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>The Elector defends his frequent complaints to the Emperor, arguing they were not acts of disobedience but necessary warnings about the total collapse of order and the devastation of the Empire by undisciplined troops.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Elector’s description of his diplomatic efforts. Having previously described the devastation of the war, he now details the specific embassies and petitions he sent to the Emperor to seek peace and address the grievances caused by the Edict of Restitution.</meta>

...has faithfully represented the situation, seeking a most gracious and prompt **remedy** <unclear>remediation</unclear>, and praying that the blessed and highly necessary **peace** <unclear>peace</unclear> might finally be restored and brought back, and an end made to the bloody war and the ruin of the land. He has most highly and movingly petitioned for this, and for that reason, in the year 1630, he sent a special and costly **embassy** <note>original: "Legation"</note> to **His Imperial Majesty** in **Vienna**. Both orally and in writing (as demonstrated by others in printed publications), the ambassadors presented a most humble **petition** <margin>N. 1.</margin> <note>original: "Memorial"</note> which detailed these matters at length. 

He also acted with great diligence, obedience, and conviction, out of a good and faithful heart, at the recently held **Convention** <note>A meeting of the Imperial Electors.</note> in **Regensburg**. There, he voiced his concerns in the public sessions and elsewhere, arguing that the grievances arising from the **Imperial Edict** issued in the year 1629 regarding **Church properties** <term>Ecclesiastical Estates</term> <note>The Edict of Restitution, which mandated the return of all church lands seized by Protestants since 1552.</note> should be remedied. 

However, as the hardships increased daily and the enforcement of the **Edict** was pursued with great zeal, even **His Electoral Serenity's** own highly deserving House was not spared. This occurred despite the fact that his beloved son, **Duke Augustus of Saxony**, had initially been legitimately nominated as the **Coadjutor** <note>An assistant bishop designated to succeed the current bishop.</note> of the Primacy and **Archbishopric of Magdeburg**, with the **promise of succession** <note>original: "cum promissione de succedendo"</note>. This nomination was made by the **Cathedral Chapter** <note>The governing body of clergy for a cathedral.</note> itself with unanimous consent, and in the same manner as had been the tradition and custom in those places for many years. **His Electoral Serenity** most humbly reported this to the **Roman Imperial Majesty**. At that time, although the matter was **known throughout the Empire** <note>original: "Reichskündig"</note>, no one contradicted it, and thereafter...

His Princely Grace <note>Referring to Duke Augustus of Saxony, mentioned on the previous page.</note> had been requested and called to the office of **Archbishop**, yet he was to be pushed aside and deposed from it entirely unheard. Alongside this, **His Electoral Serenity** <note>The Elector of Saxony, John George I.</note> was incessantly petitioned by the Protestant and Protesting Estates and most strongly reminded and exhorted of his **Electoral Word**, which he had given in the year 1620 and often thereafter. Therefore, **His Electoral Serenity** once again at length **remonstrated** <note>original: "remonstriret"; to formally present a list of grievances.</note> these matters to **His Imperial Majesty** in a most humble, obedient, and detailed letter—which was likewise later published in print—dated the 24th of August in the aforementioned year 1630. He **repeated** <note>original: "repetiret"</note> his faithful and most obedient plea for the Emperor to most graciously provide counsel and a remedy for these affairs, adding that he would ultimately be unable to avoid meeting with the suffering Estates to prevent greater disaster and holding peaceful **consultations** <note>original: "Consultationen"</note> regarding these issues.

In all of this, as he can well testify before **GOD** and men, **His Electoral Serenity** was moved by no improper thoughts to be in any way vexing to **His Imperial Majesty**, and least of all to infringe upon that high **Imperial Respect** <note>The honor and authority due to the Emperor.</note> which he has always regarded so obediently and helped to protect so faithfully. Rather, he was driven and compelled to this by the heavy duty with which

Therefore, as His Imperial Majesty’s innermost and most secret counselor, it was his duty and obligation—by virtue of his office, duty, and status, as mentioned above—to most humbly and open-heartedly remind the Emperor of these things and to consider the **welfare and preservation** <note>original: "Wolfarth vnd Conservation"</note> of the Empire. <meta>This page continues the Elector of Saxony’s formal justification to King Philip IV of Spain. From this preceding, well-founded account and what is contained in the attached supplements, Your Royal Majesty in Spain <note>King Philip IV of Spain (r. 1621–1665), a key ally of the Holy Roman Emperor</note>, according to your most enlightened royal understanding, and indeed everyone else, may now recognize how upright, how faithful, how obedient, how cautious, how respectful <note>original: *reverent*</note>, how steadfast, how entirely peaceable, and how exceedingly patient His Electoral Serenity <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony (r. 1611–1648)</note> has proven and shown himself to be in all respects throughout this highly important, great, difficult, and dangerous work; and how poorly and hostilely, by contrast, others have behaved toward His Electoral Serenity. He leaves all of this to Holy, highly-praised GOD, taking comfort in his good conscience, upright heart, and lawful actions <note>original: *Actionen*</note>, and placing firm trust once again in Divine help for a just cause. 

He is otherwise certainly grieved at heart that matters have turned out in this shattered condition and most dangerous extremities <note>original: *Extremiteten*; meaning the most dire or desperate of circumstances</note>, but he hopes to be sufficiently excused for all the disaster before GOD and all the world. Beside this, he most highly wishes for his part that such means <note>original: *Media*</note> and measures might be found and seized, through which, without delay, a general, thorough, good, secure, and lasting peace might be re-established in the Holy Roman Empire, and a good understanding and much-needed harmony <note>original: *harmoni*</note> between the Head <note>the Holy Roman Emperor, Ferdinand II</note> and the members <note>the Imperial Estates, the princes and cities that made up the Empire</note>, as well as all the Estates, be established and strengthened, thereby rescuing the suffering Empire from final ruin <note>original: *Ruin*</note> and restoring it to its former flowering <note>original: *flor*; meaning a state of blooming prosperity</note> and prosperity. For His Electoral Serenity cannot see how, given the current state of affairs and circumstances, the beloved [Fatherland], now lying in its death-throes <note>original: *in agone*; a Latin term for the final struggle before death</note>—
Va- <note>Catchword for *Vaterland* (Fatherland) on the following page.</note>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal statement by John George I, Elector of Saxony. It continues the sentiment from the previous page regarding the restoration of harmony between the Emperor and the Estates, specifically addressing the danger of individual territories making separate peace deals.</meta>

the German Fatherland might be served by separate treaties <term>original: *particular Tractaten*; separate peace agreements made by individual states rather than a collective imperial settlement</term>. The many examples that have occurred during the ongoing unrest in the Empire <note>original: *Imperio*; the Holy Roman Empire</note> these past years demonstrate that the objective of peace cannot be achieved this way. Instead, even greater unrest has arisen from such actions. Furthermore, His Electoral Serenity <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony (r. 1611–1648)</note> would not be able to justify such a course of action to His Royal Majesty in Sweden <note>King Gustavus Adolphus (r. 1611–1632), the Elector's ally who had recently won the Battle of Breitenfeld</note>, nor to his fellow Evangelical and Protestant Estates <term>original: *Mit-Stände*; the various princes and imperial cities that held status within the Empire</term>.

However, regarding what His Electoral Serenity can do to promote a general, thorough, honorable, good, secure, and lasting Universal Peace—provided there is a sincere inclination toward it—he will certainly not allow anything to be lacking on his part, in accordance with his world-renowned peaceful inclination and in all fair and possible matters. He wished to report this as a resolution to the Lord Sub-delegate <note>An official envoy or representative acting on behalf of a higher authority</note>, toward whom he remains favorably inclined with Electoral grace. 

Signed at Dresden on October 19, in the year 1631.

# Appendices.

->No. 1.<-
->Memorandum <note>original: *Memorial*; a formal diplomatic statement or petition</note> which the Ambassadors of His Electoral Serenity<-
->of Saxony, etc., delivered to His Roman<-
->Imperial Majesty in the year 1630 at Vienna.<-

Most Serene, Most Puissant, Most Unconquerable Roman Emperor, also King of Hungary and Bohemia, Most Gracious Lord. To Your Imperial Majesty, the Most Serene Elector of Saxony and Burgrave of Magdeburg, etc.—Your Imperial Majesty’s faithful and obedient Elector, our most gracious Lord—has dispatched us, his most humble servants, with specific instructions and a mission <term>original: *Werbung*; a formal diplomatic suit or proposal</term>, and therewith commanded us to offer Your Imperial Majesty the dutiful, most humble, and most obedient services of His Electoral Serenity. Furthermore, we are to report that, should Your Imperial Majesty, together with all your high-born relatives, fare happily and well in bodily health and all other matters, it would be a particular great joy for His Electoral Serenity to learn of it. He also wishes, from a most humble and faithful heart, that Almighty God would [keep] Your Imperial Majesty during these current worrisome and dangerous times,

...be served in the Fatherland of the German Nation through separate treaties <note>original: *particular Tractaten*; individual agreements between specific states rather than a comprehensive peace for the whole Empire.</note>, and the many examples that have occurred this year during the ongoing unrest in the Empire <note>original: *Imperio*; the Holy Roman Empire.</note> demonstrate that the goal of peace cannot be achieved in this way. Instead, much greater unrest has arisen from such attempts. Furthermore, His Electoral Serenity would not be able to justify such actions to His Royal Majesty in Sweden <note>Referring to King Gustavus Adolphus, who had recently entered the war as a protector of the Protestants.</note>, nor to his Evangelical and Protestant Co-Estates <term>Co-Estates; the various princes, dukes, and imperial cities that held legal status and a vote within the structures of the Holy Roman Empire.</term>.

However, regarding what His Electoral Serenity can do and perform to promote a general, thorough, honorable, good, secure, and lasting Universal Peace—provided there is a sincere inclination toward it—he will certainly, in accordance with his world-renowned peaceful *inclination* <note>original: *inclination*; a natural disposition or tendency.</note>, leave nothing undone within the realm of all fair and possible things. He has ordered this to be reported as a *Resolution* <note>original: *Resolution*; a formal decision or official answer to a diplomatic proposal.</note> to the Lord *Subdelegate* <term>Subdelegate; a subordinate official acting as a representative or negotiator on behalf of a higher authority.</term>, to whom His Electoral Grace is well-disposed. *Signed* at Dresden on the 19th of October in the year 1631.

# Appendices. <note>original: *Beylagen*; supplementary documents or annexes added to a main text.</note>

### No. I.
### Memorandum which His Electoral Serenity
### of Saxony, etc. [sent via] Envoys to the Holy
### Roman Imperial Majesty, Anno 1630,
### delivered in Vienna.

Most Serene, Most Powerful, Most Unconquerable Roman Emperor, also King of Hungary and Bohemia, Most Gracious Lord. To Your Imperial Majesty, the Most Serene Elector of Saxony and Burgrave of Magdeburg, etc., Your Imperial Majesty’s loyal and obedient Elector, our most gracious Lord, has dispatched us, his most humble servants, with certain *instruction* <note>original: *instruction*; formal diplomatic orders or briefing documents provided to an ambassador.</note> and a mission, and therewith commanded us to convey to Your Imperial Majesty His Electoral Serenity’s dutiful, most humble, and most obedient service; and furthermore to report that if Your Imperial Majesty, together with all your high relatives, should fare happily and well in bodily health and otherwise, it would be a particular and great joy for His Electoral Serenity to learn of it; Wishing also from a most humble and loyal heart that Almighty GOD would preserve Your Imperial Majesty during these current anxious and dangerous times,

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening of a 1630 diplomatic memorandum from the Elector of Saxony to the Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II. It uses highly stylized, respectful language typical of 17th-century court diplomacy to establish a loyal tone before addressing the grievances of the Protestant states.</summary>

<keywords>Elector of Saxony, John George I, Holy Roman Emperor, Ferdinand II, Vienna, diplomacy, 1630, Thirty Years' War, formal address</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal address from the Elector of Saxony's envoys to Emperor Ferdinand II. The word fragment "ten" at the start completes the prayer for the Emperor's preservation found on the previous page.</meta>

...preserve and maintain Your Imperial Majesty for the benefit of the **Holy Roman Empire** for many long years in constant physical health, a fortunate and peaceful reign, and all Imperial prosperity. 

Upon this, they initially and foremost testified and affirmed with heart and voice their most loyal affection, love, obedience, and devotion, by which they are most faithfully devoted to Your Imperial Majesty as their God-appointed head; and that they intend, through Divine grant, to remain upright, steadfast, and firm in this devotion at all times until their blessed end. They also noted that Your Imperial Majesty is quite reluctant to burden them further amidst the high and heavy Imperial affairs already weighing upon you. 

However, His Electoral Serenity <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony (reigned 1611–1656), a key figure in the Thirty Years' War.</note> must not only personally behold, with a most sorrowful spirit and great lamentation, the utterly pitiable, wretched, shattered, highly distressed, and dangerous condition in which the **Holy Roman Empire of the German Nation**, alas, finds itself; but he is also approached, respectively <note>original: *respective*</note>, by both the Lord **Elector of Brandenburg** and other distinguished Princes, Estates, and members of the Empire with many and incessant complaints and lamentations. Furthermore, he is quite movingly reminded of his supporting Electoral Office, the Fundamental Laws, the **Imperial Constitutions**, and other wholesome ordinances, as well as by the Estates of the **Upper Saxon Circle** <note>The *Ober Sächsischer Kreis* was an administrative grouping of states in the northeast of the Empire responsible for defense and tax collection.</note> regarding his office as **Circle Colonel** <note>term: *Kreisobrist*; the military official responsible for the defense and mobilization of an Imperial Circle.</note>. Consequently, His Electoral Serenity—no less because of the heavy duty by which he is obligated to Your Imperial Majesty and the Holy Roman Empire—likewise...

<summary>The Elector of Saxony’s envoys express their unwavering loyalty to the Emperor before describing the "pitiable and wretched" state of the Empire during the war. The Elector emphasizes his obligation to intervene, spurred by pleas from the Elector of Brandenburg and his own duties as a regional military leader.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the formal appeal from the Elector of Saxony to the Emperor. The previous page ended by discussing the Elector's obligations to the Imperial Constitutions and his office as a Prince of the Empire.</meta>

<sig>D ij</sig>

[stirrings] of his Christian conscience, as much because of the high and most humble love and loyalty which they have at all times borne toward Your Imperial Majesty, as well as Your entire most highly honored **Archducal House of Austria** <note>The House of Habsburg, the ruling dynasty of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, and still bear in a completely unfeigned manner. They have also proven themselves steadfast and unchanging, faithfully risking their own bodies, property, and blood, lands and people, high status and dignities—and indeed, they did so at that time when, in human eyes, everything stood in the highest and most extreme danger and a doubtful outcome, when rescue was necessary, and when unfeigned loyalty and love could be truly recognized. 

Furthermore, because his incumbent office binds and directs him to faithfully care for the salvation, benefit, honor, reputation, and welfare of Your Imperial Majesty and the **Holy Roman Empire**, and to preserve the dear Peace—as a noble, glorious jewel and the most prominent part of a fortunate reign—and to be a comfort to all Christian people, he could not omit sending this most humble and obedient embassy to Your Imperial Majesty. 

He does so with the most humble prayer that Your Imperial Majesty would, according to your world-renowned Imperial clemency, mildness, and goodness, not only take notice of this in Imperial Grace, but also—as a most just, kind, and highly praiseworthy Emperor—most graciously and completely provide relief without delay to the complaining, unheard-of tribulations, hardships, and misery. May you [refresh] the suffering, agonizing **Roman Empire** with the lovely [peace], [sought] by so many thousands of oppressed and [anxious]— <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "ängstig-" suggests the next page begins with a word describing the fearful or anxious state of the people.</note>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the petition from the Elector of Saxony to the Holy Roman Emperor. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the hope for a refreshing peace to rescue the Empire from ruin.</meta>

—and [refresh] those anxious people with the most highly desired, general, thorough, lasting, honorable, sincere, and universal peace, and thereby rescue them from final and total ruin.

For it is most graciously known to Your **Imperial Majesty** <note>original: "E. Keys. Mayst."</note> yourself what misery, lamentation, need, fear, desolation, and devastation has accrued to the **Holy Empire** <note>The Holy Roman Empire.</note> because of the war-uprisings and unrest that arose within it and have continued for so many long years. It is known how this flame of war has not only seized one kingdom, land, and place, but has spread through the entire Empire and consumed and corrupted one province—indeed, one **Imperial Circle** <note>term: Circle (Kreis). A regional administrative grouping of states within the Holy Roman Empire.</note>—after another. This has gained the upper hand to such an extent that almost everything has been turned to ashes, and unfortunately, the few remains continue to burn in bright flames. It is to be feared that if a speedy rescue does not occur, everything might be completely ruined to the irrecoverable damage of the **Holy Empire** and the inextinguishable mockery of the **German Nation**, which is otherwise praised throughout the world.

His **Electoral Highness** <note>original: "Ihre Churf. Durchl." Referring here to the Elector of Saxony.</note> had indeed stood in the certain and indubitable hope that—after those magnificent and glorious victories granted by God the Almighty to Your **Imperial Majesty**, and because the minds inclined toward unrest were subdued, the disorder in the **Lower Saxon Circle** stilled, and a complete peace established with the **Royal Dignity in Denmark** <note>Refers to the Treaty of Lübeck (1629) which ended Danish intervention in the Thirty Years' War.</note>—this lamentation might be somewhat checked. He hoped the faithful **Estates** <note>The princes, nobles, and imperial cities that held a seat in the Imperial Diet.</note>, after enduring such manifold and unheard-of misery and sadness, would be comforted again, that blessed peace would be restored, and the **Imperial Constitutions** <note>The fundamental laws and legal framework of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> [returned] to their [powers]—

<meta>This page continues the petition from the Elector of Saxony to the Holy Roman Emperor, following the previous page's discussion on the hope for a general peace to save the German Nation from ruin.</meta>

—have been brought to power and effect. This is especially true because the highly respected **Electoral College** <note>term: Electoral College (Kurfürstenrat). The elite group of princes responsible for electing the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> most humbly reminded and petitioned Your **Imperial Majesty** <note>original: "E. Keys. Mayst."</note> regarding this matter from Mühlhausen in the year 1627. At that time, they provided a detailed demonstration of how things were faring everywhere in the **Empire** <note>original: "Imperio"</note> and in what an evil state it was found. His **Electoral Highness** <note>Referring to the Elector of Saxony.</note> also personally, on several occasions, most faithfully and movingly made known to Your **Imperial Majesty**, as well as to his fellow **Electors**, the extreme need and highest danger to be feared. He did so with lengthy explanations and in a most humble and friendly manner **respectively** <note>original: "respective"</note>, diligently asking and urging for a remedy. And though they knew Your **Imperial Majesty’s** most just and peace-loving heart and mind were **inclined** <note>original: "inclinirt"</note> toward this in an Imperial fashion, they nevertheless had to see with the greatest sadness—and even partially experience for themselves—that the need, misery, and **oppressions** <note>original: "pressuren"</note> have instead grown from day to day, and have almost never been worse or more dangerous than they are now.

The complaints of His **Electoral Highness** the Elector of Brandenburg reveal the condition in which the **Electorate of Brandenburg** currently finds itself. It is still occupied by many troops and burdened with heavy **levies** <note>original: "Contribution." Forced financial payments demanded by occupying or passing armies.</note>; for instance, the city of **Frankfurt an der Oder** is still expected to pay 9,000 **Reichsthaler** <note>term: Reichsthaler. The standard large silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire.</note> monthly. The land is oppressed by many troop crossings and other **burdens** <note>original: "pressuren"</note>, and is so ruined and battered that it no longer resembles an Electorate at all. This treatment of a loyal and obedient Elector of the Empire is a matter of very grave and far-reaching concern.

<meta>This page continues the petition detailing the suffering of the Elector of Saxony's relatives and the Protestant estates due to the heavy burden of quartering troops during the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

How things fared for His **Electoral Highness’s** <note>original: "Seiner Churf. Durchl." Referring to the Elector of Saxony.</note> beloved cousins—the Dukes of **Saxony**, **Altenburg**, **Weimar**, **Coburg**, and **Eisenach**—is sadly evident to all and can be seen in their sorrowful complaints. In those places, everything is being laid waste, and the lands are so sucked dry that even Their **Princely Graces** <note>original: "Ihre F. F. G. G. D. D. D. D." This repetitive abbreviation signifies the multiple dukes mentioned previously.</note> are scarcely able to maintain themselves at their own tables. Indeed, the *insolence* of the **soldiery** <note>original: "Soldatesca." A term often used to describe undisciplined or predatory groups of soldiers.</note> has gained such a foothold that **quarters** <note>original: "Quartier." The forced housing and feeding of soldiers by civilians or local lords.</note> are changed at will; and because nothing more can be found among the impoverished, they pressure the lords themselves, hinting that *ruin* and plundering may follow. Indeed, they even attack them with heavy accusations, as recently happened to the **Duke of Altenburg’s** Princely Grace.

Likewise, the **Schönburg Company** of the **Catholic** troops has remained stationed with His Princely Grace, the **Duke of Weimar**, for nearly three years now, without moving a foot, causing the uttermost destruction. And His Princely Grace is expected to take in even more **Imperial troops** as soon as these depart, as is currently intended with **Colonel Holck’s** <note>term: Colonel Holck (Henrik Holck). A prominent commander in the Imperial army known for his raids and recruitment efforts during this period.</note> newly recruited companies. This is despite the obvious and most distressing *impossibility* <note>original: "impossibilet." The author uses a Latinized term to emphasize the total lack of resources.</note> of it, and the fact that the misery and wretchedness among the poor people cannot be sufficiently expressed in words.

In what a most lamentable and pitiful state the Princes and **Estates** <note>term: Estates (Stände). The territories or social ranks that had the right to be represented in the Imperial Diet.</note> of the **Augsburg Confession** <note>term: Augsburg Confession. The founding document of the Lutheran faith; here it identifies the Protestant princes.</note> in the noble **Franconian** and **Swabian Circles** <note>term: Imperial Circles (Reichskreise). Administrative districts of the Holy Roman Empire responsible for defense and tax collection.</note> find themselves—especially the Lord **Margrave’s**...

Margrave **Christian of Brandenburg**, the **Duke of Württemberg**, and the **Margrave of Baden** <note>original: "F. F. F. G. G. G." Abbreviation for 'Their Princely Graces'.</note>, together with their respective loyal lands and subjects, have fallen into a state of service and bondage through the manifold, continuous, and unbearable **pressures of war** <note>original: "Kriegs-pressuren." This refers to the systemic burden placed on civilian populations to support active armies.</note>—which have continued without measure, number, or end—as well as through forced, heavy, and unattainable **monetary exactions** <note>original: "Geld exactionen." Forced levies of money.</note>, **contributions** <note>term: contributions. Regular payments demanded by occupying or passing armies for their upkeep, often under threat of violence.</note>, and other fundamentally ruinous, cruel, and terrifying tribulations, burdens, and anxieties never heard of before. They remain miserably oppressed, as appears from their various most humble complaints brought before **Your Imperial Majesty** <note>original: "E. Keys. Mayst." (Eure Kayserliche Mayestät).</note>, both in writing and through envoys. His **Electoral Highness** <note>original: "Seiner Churf. Durchl." Referring to the Elector of Saxony.</note> was made aware of such matters by Their Princely Graces quite movingly and sorrowfully through special envoys shortly before this **mission** <note>original: "Legation." A diplomatic mission or embassy.</note>.

In the **Duchy of Braunschweig** <note>original: "Braunschweig." Known in English as Brunswick.</note>, both lords and subjects sigh; and things fare so miserably for His **Princely Grace** <note>original: "F. Gnad."</note>, despite his significant lands, that they almost do not know where they shall find the means to survive.

The **Duchy of Lüneburg** also complains not a little, and destruction has struck His **Princely Grace's** land more than is right.

What misery exists in the **Duchy of Pomerania** is, alas, more to be lamented than can be detailed at length. The need and the misery speak for themselves; His **Princely Grace** has himself most humbly demonstrated and presented this in detail to **Your Imperial Majesty** on several occasions.

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the report on the devastation of the Holy Roman Empire during the Thirty Years' War. Having just mentioned the Duchy of Brunswick, the text now focuses on the desperate situation in Pomerania and Hesse, describing the breakdown of civil administration and the seizure of strategic fortifications by military forces.</meta>

There, one found nothing but pure devastation, hunger, and sorrow—such misery that it could not be recounted without the deepest sadness. It had reached the point where, contrary to nature and love, inhuman deeds were committed to satisfy hunger <note>This likely refers to contemporary reports of starvation so extreme that people resorted to desperate or "unnatural" acts, a common theme in the chronicles of the 1630s.</note>. Likewise, matters reached such a state that because the Princely Grace of the Duke of Pomerania could no longer keep up with the monetary demands <note>original: "mit dem Gelde nicht mehr verfolgen könten," referring to the inability to pay "contributions" or war taxes to the occupying armies.</note>, he was forced to sign over and grant administrative offices <term>Aempter: local administrative and tax-collecting districts that provided revenue</term> to the military officers. 

A complaint regarding this was brought before His Electoral Highness, the Lord Elector of Brandenburg, specifically concerning how this had allegedly occurred with the district of Klempenaw <note>modern: Klempenow</note>. Indeed, His Princely Grace was pressured so severely that the passes of Damm, Greifenhagen, and Gartz <note>Strategic fortified towns in Pomerania along the Oder River: modern Szczecin-Dąbie, Gryfino, and Gartz</note> were demanded from him, even though they lay deep within the territory and thus faced no danger from the enemy <note>original: "Wiederpart"</note> through either trickery or force. Furthermore, His Princely Grace—acting as a loyal, steadfast, obedient, and devoted Prince of the Empire—had offered and pledged to continue guarding and preserving them well, as he had done until now. Yet, these locations were still demanded from him; should this be carried out by force, His Princely Grace would be left completely blockaded in his own residence and stripped of all means of support.

How the Landgraviate of Hesse has been ravaged on all sides is equally evident to the eye. It has now borne the burden [of war] for many long years, and—quite apart from the physical devastation—it has cost many millions, as seen in the case of the Princely Grace of Landgrave Wilhelm...

All <term>Commercia: commerce and trade</term>, business, and dealings are simultaneously lying in ruin, whereas they ought to be flourishing in every place. <meta>This page continues the legal arguments regarding the religious rights of noble families whose territories have been divided. It follows a discussion on whether a prince loses his right to practice his faith if his lands are partitioned among heirs.</meta>

<sig>G ij</sig>

born **Princes of the Empire** <note>original: *Reichs-Fürsten*; high-ranking nobles who held their lands directly from the Emperor</note>, being immediately eligible for the benefits of the **Peace of Religion** <note>The Peace of Augsburg (1555), which gave princes the right to choose the religion of their territory</note>, are not deprived of their princely dignity and **immunities** by the division of their lands. Instead, the status and dignity of the person must be considered above all else <note>original: *qualitas & dignitas personæ considerirt*</note>. Such a division could not be interpreted in a way that would deny or cut off the free <term>Practice of Religion</term> <note>original: *Exercitium*; the right to hold religious services</note> for their court, officials, servants, and dependents. This is well known and has never been experienced otherwise in the Empire; the historical examples—and especially what transpired regarding the **Count of Oldenburg**—prove the contrary <note>original: *in contrarium*</note>.

In what manner the city of **Schweinfurt**—regarding its parish church and the **Carmelite Monastery**—as well as several other cities, have been pressured in such cases despite their firmly grounded authority, can be seen in detail from the complaints most humbly submitted to Your **Imperial Majesty**. Now, however, through these grave and far-reaching proceedings and dangerous discourse, mistrust—which is the most harmful plague of public affairs <note>original: *nocentissima Pestis Rerum publicarum*</note>—is noticeably increased and minds are inflamed. Your Imperial Majesty must, in your own most gracious and highly enlightened understanding, judge where this may lead and to what ultimate extremes it might finally break out if a cessation of these actions does not occur. If they instead proceed with such harshness, the **Estates** <note>The various territories and authorities represented in the Imperial Diet</note> will be so oppressed and such dangerous, far-reaching disputes will be awakened that even the **Evangelical citizenry** of **Augsburg** would be distressed, contrary to the clear, plain letter of the Peace of Religion.

<meta>This page continues the legal and diplomatic appeal to the Emperor. Following the discussion of the religious rights of divided noble territories, the text now transitions to the broader constitutional significance of the Imperial Peaces (Religious and Secular) as the bedrock of the Empire’s survival.</meta>

The **Religious and Secular Peaces** of the Holy Roman Empire were the *adamantine pillars and bonds* <note>original: *adamantina fulcra & vincula*</note> upon which the *safety and tranquility of the Empire* <note>original: *incolumitas & tranquillitas Imperij*</note> rested and through which it would be preserved. These were indissoluble bonds, to which both the Head <note>the Emperor</note> and the members <note>the Estates/Princes</note> were bound by most solemn oath. Against these, no "declaration" (as mentioned above) should have any standing; rather, they were entirely forbidden *by the addition of a nullifying clause* <note>original: *additâ clausulâ cassatoriâ*; a legal provision that voids any acts or decrees that contradict the treaty</note>.

That His **Electoral Highness** <term>Electoral Highness</term> <note>original: *Churf. Durchl.* (Churfürstliche Durchlaucht); the title for one of the high-ranking princes who elected the Emperor</note> should now hold so firmly to these, as well as to the **Fundamental Laws** and all other beneficial Imperial **Constitutions**, and should most humbly point out the wretched state of the Holy Roman Empire and what disruptions are occurring within it—as well as what disaster and *fatal ruin* <note>original: *exitial ruin*</note> could follow from them, which indeed stand as if at the door—and should warn of all this so faithfully, and also so Christianly advocate for his **fellow believers** <note>original: *ReligionsVerwandten*; those sharing the same confession, likely the Lutheran or Reformed faith</note>, for this His Electoral Highness cannot be held in suspicion. To Your **Imperial Majesty** <term>Imperial Majesty</term> <note>original: *E. Keys. Mayst.* (Eure Keyserliche Mayestät)</note>, as his appointed head in this world, His Electoral Highness acknowledges himself duty-bound to sincerely show all most humble obedience, **respect**, honor, love, and loyalty.

On the other hand, however, His Electoral Highness must and will remain steadfast toward Holy God in the confession of His teaching, honor, and Word, and give to His most holy name what is due to Him in most dutiful devotion and a Christian spirit; and to remain firm in this on all sides until his blessed end, and further, to lead his beloved Electoral children, bestowed by God, toward this in a fatherly manner...

<summary>The author argues that the Religious and Secular Peaces are the unbreakable foundations of the Empire's stability and warns that ignoring them will lead to total ruin. He balances his political loyalty to the Emperor with his ultimate religious duty to God and the protection of his Protestant heirs.</summary>

<keywords>Peace of Religion, Holy Roman Empire, Imperial Constitution, Electoral Highness, Religious Liberty, Nullifying Clause, political stability, Protestantism</keywords>

# No. 2.

### Letter from His Electoral Serenity of Saxony, etc. <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony (1585–1656). "Electoral Serenity" was the formal title for the princes who held the right to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>
->to the Imperial Majesty, dated August 24, 1630.<-

Most Gracious Lord,

That **Your Imperial Majesty** has most graciously received my most humble letter of notification concerning the invasion of the **Royal Majesty of Sweden** <note>King Gustavus Adolphus of Sweden, who had landed in Pomerania in July 1630, bringing the Protestant Swedish army into the conflict.</note> into the territory of the Empire, as well as my most obedient and heartfelt reminder and petition attached to it; for this I most humbly thank **Your Imperial Majesty**. I can also testify that it was intended by me in no other way than most faithfully and well. I was driven to this by the heavy duty to which I am bound to **Your Imperial Majesty** and the **Holy Roman Empire**, as well as my holding of the **Electoral Office**, and also the special love I bear toward **Your Imperial Majesty** and your **Most Honored Archducal House** <note>The House of Habsburg, the ruling dynasty of the Empire.</note>. Driven by these same causes and by constant devotion, I also most humbly send to **Your Imperial Majesty** the enclosed [report regarding] what Lord **Margrave Christian Wilhelm of Brandenburg**, Your Grace <note>original: *L.*, an abbreviation for *Liebden*, a formal honorific used between high-ranking nobles meaning "Your Dearness" or "Your Grace".</note> [has done]

upon their announcement that they have moved into the **Archbishopric of Magdeburg** <note>The *Ertzstifft Magdeburgk* was a significant ecclesiastical territory. Christian Wilhelm, the former administrator, was attempting to reclaim it with Swedish support.</note> and furthermore made a request to me, [I] have provided a reply. Otherwise, alongside **Your Imperial Majesty**, I justly lament most regretfully that the beloved Fatherland of the **German Nation** has now burned for twelve years in such a great fiery flame of bloody, destructive war. Through this, not only many beautiful cities, castles, market towns, and provinces, but entire Electorates and Principalities have been miserably ruined, and some even completely reduced to ashes. Furthermore, alongside this evil, all well-conceived and wholesome, and so highly sworn **fundamental laws**, **Imperial Constitutions**, and **District Ordinances** <note>original: *Kreiß Ordnungen*. <meta>This page continues the Elector of Saxony’s formal grievance to Emperor Ferdinand II. The Elector is arguing that the current military impositions and financial demands are illegal under the laws of the Holy Roman Empire. ...for an outcome <note>original: *Außgang*; a resolution or way out of the current crisis.</note>—especially since fickle Fortune—which is particularly changeable in **matters of war** <term>Kriegssachen: the legal and logistical affairs related to the conduct of the ongoing Thirty Years' War.</term> and tends to change the situation a thousand times over in every single moment <note>original: *per mille vices in singula momenta*</note>—might hide itself away.

<meta>This page continues the Protestant Estates' legal defense against the enforcement of the Edict of Restitution. It transitions from citing historical precedents (Augsburg and Speyer) to arguing that because the religious laws are currently under dispute (and thus "uncertain"), they cannot be legally enforced until a mutual understanding is reached. It cannot be denied by anyone that for many long years now / because of these points / disputes have existed between the **Catholic and Protestant Estates** <term>Estates: The "Stände" were the constituent states of the Holy Roman Empire, including princes, dukes, and imperial cities, who held legal standing under the Emperor.</term> / and how it has been held by the Catholics that there was a disagreement **concerning a doubtful interpretation** <note>original Latin: "super dubio intellectu"</note> / and each side has brought forward and presented its reasons regarding this. Therefore, this has been recognized at all times not only by the Estates themselves / but also by the previous most highly praiseworthy **Roman Emperors** / as the **Imperial Acts** <note>original German: "Reichsacta"; the official records and archives of the Imperial Diet's proceedings.</note> testify in greater detail. And if these disputes are now to be brought to a resolution / it will / according to their nature and character / be necessary in every way that this occurs in the manner customary within the **Roman Empire**. Accordingly, the **Evangelical and Protestant Estates** cannot concede anything else / and therefore should not be burdened with any **executions** <term>Executions: In this legal context, "Executionen" refers to the forced military or administrative enforcement of an imperial decree or judgment.</term> / but rather have on their side the well-known **legal maxim**: **"Only that which is certain ought to be brought to execution; what is uncertain, however, must be left for further examination."** <note>original Latin: "Illud solum, quod certum est, ad executionem trahi oportere; incertum verò ulterius examinandum relinqui."</note>

What also to Your Imperial Majesty I / **Count Palatine Augustus** <note>Augustus, Count Palatine of Sulzbach (1582–1632), a notable Lutheran prince.</note> / for myself / and for my brother, the **Palatine**...

<meta>This page continues the legal and political grievances of the Protestant estates presented to the Emperor. petitions made / likewise regarding the **point of confiscation of property** / and how they could not grant such a thing to the legal disadvantage <note>original: "præjudiz"</note> of themselves and other Estates of the Empire / as set out with solid legal foundations / the writings demonstrate. oppressed in their consciences, and are to be deprived of that noble jewel, 
**German Liberty** <note>original: "Teutschen Freyheit"; the traditional legal rights and autonomy of the imperial princes against the centralized power of the Emperor.</note>. 

Thus, it may now almost be said of agonizing Germany what

*that she daily buys her servitude, and daily feeds it* <note>original: "quòd servitutem suam qvotidie emat, qvotidie pascat." A quote from Tacitus’s *Agricola* regarding how the Britons were forced to provide for the Roman armies that oppressed them.</note>. 

And although all of this is attempted to be excused in part by the 
pretended **case of necessity** <term>case of necessity <note>original: "casu necessitatis"; a legal justification used to bypass constitutional laws or the consent of the Estates in times of war.</note></term>,

For Your Imperial Majesty must conclude for yourself that—just as I, the **Elector of Saxony** <term>One of the highest-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire with the right to elect the Emperor.</term>, have already previously declared, and do hereby once again declare in most humble respect, that I could not endure or permit such things for the sake of my lands and people—likewise I, the **Elector of Brandenburg**, as well as We, the other Princes and present **Estates** <term>The "Stände" or Estates were the territories and authorities that had a seat and vote in the Imperial Diet.</term>, can no longer at all endure such pressures because of their manifest impossibility.

We also do not know at all how we could answer for such things before **God** and worthy **posterity**, nor how we could permit our subjects to be used for these purposes. And if we, contrary to all hope, [are further burdened] by the **soldiery** <note>original: "Soldatesca"; a term often used to describe unruly or undisciplined troops.</note>...

## of the Resolution,
### Which His Electoral Highness of Saxony etc. gave to the Imperial Envoy on May 20, the year 1631.

Regarding what was brought forward and presented orally on behalf of the **Roman Imperial Majesty** by his highly distinguished envoy—the noble and steadfast Lord **Hans Ruprecht Hegenmüller** of and at Dubenweiler, on Albrechtsberg, His Imperial Majesty’s **Privy Councilor** and **Land Under-Marshal** <term>original: "LandvnterMarschalch"; a high-ranking administrative and judicial official responsible for regional governance</term> of the **Archduchy of Austria below the Enns** <note>A historical region roughly corresponding to modern-day Lower Austria.</note>—to the most serene **Elector of Saxony** and **Burgrave of Magdeburg** etc., and what was further submitted as a written excerpt of the most gracious instructions he received: His Electoral Highness has listened to this with due reverence, has further diligently read it, and has maturely considered it.

His Electoral Highness expresses his most humble and obedient thanks to His Imperial Majesty for the most graciously delivered Imperial greeting, favor, and grace, for the most gracious inquiry into his condition, and for the good wishes attached to it. And just as His Electoral Highness perceives therein His Imperial Majesty's singular love and affection: he thus most humbly prays that His Imperial Majesty would also in this...

<meta>This page continues the formal response from John George I, Elector of Saxony, to the Imperial envoy Hans Ruprecht Hegenmüller. ...persist most graciously; and the Lord Envoy would know how to report most submissively what His Electoral Highness has once again declared regarding his most humble, obedient, constant loyalty and **devotion** <note>original: *devotion*</note>. *III.* The **constitution of defense** <term>original: "Defensions-verfassung"; the agreement among Protestant princes at the Leipzig Convention to raise their own defensive army</term> planned by the Evangelical and Protestant Estates.

<meta>This page continues the legal and diplomatic justification provided by the Elector of Saxony and other regional rulers to the Emperor. Your **Electoral Serenity** <note>referring to the Elector of Saxony</note>, along with the other **Estates** <term>Stände; the various territories and rulers who held a seat in the Imperial Diet</term>, could not and should not have imagined that His **Imperial Majesty** <note>the Holy Roman Emperor</note> would take their actions poorly in the slightest. Since they have the laws, the **Imperial Ordinance** <term>Reichs-Ordnung; the fundamental legal framework of the Empire</term>, established tradition, and even His Imperial Majesty’s own most gracious **approval** <note>original: "adprobation"</note> and highly esteemed Imperial word on their side, they did not believe he would be moved to act in such a way against Your Electoral Serenity.

Therefore, Your Electoral Serenity cannot fathom how this agreement and resolution—made in accordance with all Divine, International, Natural, and written laws, as well as the Imperial Constitutions and traditions—could or should be regarded as a "new alliance." This is especially true since it was made in the **execution** <note>original: "Execution"; the formal implementation or enforcement of a law</note> of the 1555 **General Imperial Recess** <term>Reichs-Abschied; the final official summary of decisions made at an Imperial Diet, specifically referring here to the landmark Religious Peace of Augsburg</term>. This arrangement, as mentioned, was permitted and allowed, and was also expressly conditioned upon remaining in **devotion** <note>original: "devotion"</note>, loyalty, and obedience to His Imperial Majesty. It was intended to offend no one, but rather to stay strictly within the boundaries of written law, the Imperial Constitution, and the **Circle and Execution Ordinances** <term>Kreyß- und Execution-Ordnung; the regulations governing the ten administrative Circles and the military enforcement of Imperial law</term>.

May Your Imperial Majesty graciously take to your Imperial heart what would finally become of the loyal **Electors** <term>Chur-Fürsten; the elite group of princes responsible for electing the Emperor</term> and Estates if, in this case, the laws, Imperial Constitutions, traditions, rightful liberties, and *the most recent legal precedents* <note>original: "novissima Exempla"</note> were of no use to them, nor could they make use of them. Indeed, it would be a grave matter if even the right of **Natural Defense** <note>original: "Natürliche Defension"; the legal concept of the inherent right to self-preservation</note> to protect oneself against prohibited violence were to be cut off from them.
