--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the diplomatic argument from the Elector of Saxony. Having previously argued that his own recent defensive actions do not constitute a "new alliance," he now discusses his historical opposition to formal partisan alliances like the Protestant Union and the Catholic League, arguing that the Empire's own laws should be sufficient to maintain peace.</meta>

<meta>catchword: on</meta>

Your **Electoral Serenity** <note>referring to the Elector of Saxony</note>, following the example of your most praiseworthy ancestors, have at all times guarded yourself against forming partisan alliances. It is well known how faithfully you warned against such things whenever they were proposed by one side or another, and how steadfastly you have conducted yourself in this matter. Your Electoral Serenity would also wish from the heart that such factions had never broken out in the **Empire** <note>original: "Imperio"; the Holy Roman Empire</note>, and that all parties had remained as strictly by the **Imperial Ordinances** <term>Reichssatzungen; the constitutional laws of the Holy Roman Empire</term> as is now being done by the **Lutherans** <note>original: "Evangelischen"</note> and **Protestants**. If everyone had been satisfied with the **Imperial Bonds** <note>original: "Reichs-Vinculis"; the legal and political ties that held the various states of the Empire together</note>, the state of affairs would certainly be much better and more fortunate than the miserable situation we unfortunately see before our eyes today.

When the **Protestant Union** <note>original: "Union"; a coalition of Protestant states formed in 1608</note> began to form, and subsequently the Catholic side—as reason teaches—created a **Counter-Alliance** <note>original: "GegenBund"; referring to the Catholic League formed in 1609</note> for their own protection (as evidenced by their public writings and other records), Your Electoral Serenity heard of this with great concern. As a wise ruler, you saw clearly what harmful disaster would follow from it. Even the late and most praiseworthy Emperors, **Rudolf** <note>Rudolf II, r. 1576–1612</note> and **Matthias** <note>r. 1612–1619</note>, themselves most graciously held the opinion that it would be best for both organizations to be dissolved and for everyone to simply abide by the **Imperial Laws** <note>original: "Reichs-Gesetze"</note>. Since the Union has long since been **dissolved** <note>original: "dissolvirt"</note>, it would have been very beneficial for the Holy Roman Empire and the common peace, as well as only fair, if the Catholic Counter-alliance had also ceased to exist. This sentiment was conveyed to the Elector of **Mainz** <note>The Archbishop-Elector of Mainz, the highest-ranking prince of the Empire</note> etc. and Your Electoral Serenity of **Saxony** etc.

<summary>The Elector of Saxony argues that partisan alliances like the Protestant Union and Catholic League have harmed the Empire, suggesting that peace is best maintained through existing Imperial laws rather than private military blocs.</summary>
<keywords>Elector of Saxony, Protestant Union, Catholic League, Imperial Law, Rudolf II, Emperor Matthias, Elector of Mainz, alliances, religious conflict</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and historical defense presented by the Elector of Saxony. Having previously discussed his general opposition to partisan factions, he now cites the specific precedents from the 1623 Regensburg Convention and a 1574 letter from Elector August to justify why the Protestant Estates might be compelled to form their own defensive alliances if the Catholic side does the same.</meta>

at the convention held at **Regensburg** in the year 1623, <note>a meeting of the Imperial Electors during the Thirty Years' War</note> [your Serenity] very movingly reminded them—citing many impressive motives and especially with this addition—that finally the **Protesting Estates** <note>the Protestant members of the Imperial Diet</note> would take cause, based on the very same foundation that prompted the **Catholic** lords to form their league, to enter into a closer organization and understanding, following the example of what they [the Catholics] had done. 

The wisdom, Imperial experience, bravery, and also the peace-loving disposition of Your **Electoral Serenity’s** great-grandfather, **Elector August** <note>Augustus, Elector of Saxony (1526–1586), known for his administrative reforms</note> of highly praiseworthy memory, as well as the constant obedience and respect that his late Electoral Grace always showed toward the **Imperial Majesty** <note>the Holy Roman Emperor</note>, is well known. Nevertheless, he stated in a detailed letter to his fellow Electors, dated **Glücksburg**, September 30, 1574, that if the **Estates of the Augsburg Confession** <term>the Lutherans, named after their 1530 statement of faith</term> were certain that the Catholic Estates had separated themselves from them in some way, and had allied themselves with foreign powers—even if only for the sake of **defense** <note>original: "Defension"</note>—they would undoubtedly consider a counter-defense and would also create and establish a similar **Counter-Alliance**. 

From this it clearly appears that the highly wise and faithful Elector did not consider such an action to be an irresponsible undertaking deserving of harsh **Mandates** <note>official imperial decrees or orders carrying legal penalties</note>. And because the **Imperial Estates** <note>original: "ReichsStände"; the rulers and entities with a seat and vote in the Imperial Diet</note> of all sides, Catholic and Protestant alike, are included in equal freedom by virtue of the highly solemnized **Fundamental and Imperial Laws**, and therefore whatever is permitted to one party...

<summary>The text argues that Protestant states have a historical and legal right to form defensive alliances if Catholic states do so first, citing a 1574 letter from Elector August of Saxony as a precedent. It emphasizes that such "counter-alliances" are a matter of equal freedom under the laws of the Holy Roman Empire.</summary>

<keywords>Regensburg Convention 1623, Elector August of Saxony, Augsburg Confession, Counter-Alliance, Imperial Estates, Fundamental Laws, religious diplomacy, Thirty Years' War</keywords>

Regarding what transpired in the **Lower Saxon Circle** <term>one of the ten administrative groupings of states within the Holy Roman Empire, which had recently faced military turmoil</term>, His Electoral Serenity remembers it quite well. ...committed by members of the alliance <note>original: "be-nißleutien," likely "Bündnißleuten"</note>, and how such things could be restrained neither by the **Colonels** <term>Obristen: high-ranking military officers</term> nor by any other means. How this mockery, scorn, and damage pained His Electoral Serenity, and how deeply it went to his heart, Your **Imperial Majesty** could most graciously judge for yourself. 

In **Lusatia** <note>a region to the east of Saxony, then under the Elector's administration</note> they also behaved very badly; whole troops of over 100 horsemen and others invaded more than once, despoiling everything in the countryside, even a small town, and moreover letting out many impudent words. 

Now, His Electoral Serenity knew no other way to oppose such **acts of violence** <note>original: "GewaltThaten"</note>—since respect among the undisciplined **soldiery** <term>Soldatesca: an unruly or lawless military force</term> has vanished—than to lawfully resist with recruited men. 

His Electoral Serenity has at all times placed his electoral trust in Your **Imperial Majesty’s** Imperial words, and held them as a strong wall and an invincible fortress, and he remains firmly assured of them once more. However, because the **soldiery** would no longer allow themselves to be turned away, and the **Colonels** and commanders could not keep them entirely in check—as they themselves indicated—one should protect oneself against this impudent company...

His **Electoral Serenity** <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony</note> most submissively and obediently

highly enlightened Imperial understanding should consider that 
if the obedient **Estates** were to be further oppressed in this way,

it might finally lead to a state of despe<unclear>ra</unclear>tion <note>original: "Desperation"; implying a total breakdown of social order or a hopeless rebellion</note>, 
which God graciously forbid. His **Electoral Serenity** could 
finally let the entire **Holy Roman Empire** and the honorable world 
judge the actions and counsels he has undertaken 
during his now nearly twenty-year **Electoral reign**.

His **Electoral Serenity** considers the amicable intervention <term>Interposition: diplomatic mediation</term> 
regarding the **King in Swe<unclear>den</unclear>** <note>Gustavus Adolphus</note> to be highly necessary, 
for the reasons already touched upon by Your **Imperial Majesty** yourself.

But because the entire highly-laudable **Electoral College** <term>Churfürstliche Collegium: the group of high-ranking princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor</term> had already intervened in this matter, it would have been proper for His **Electoral Serenity** <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony</note> to wait and see what further might be done. <meta>This page continues the personal letter from John George I, Elector of Saxony, to Emperor Ferdinand II, dated May 20, 1631. It picks up from the catchword "Liebe" (Devotion/Love) at the end of the previous page.</meta>

Devotion and constant loyalty acknowledged toward Your **Imperial Majesty** and your entire **Archducal House** <note>The House of Habsburg, the ruling dynasty of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, I have shown such [loyalty] uprightly throughout my days, and have allowed nothing to be more important or closer to my heart than to perform good, most humble, and most obedient service for Your **Imperial Majesty**. Consequently, it grieves me not a little that I must live to see what is now happening to me, and I therefore most humbly and obediently petition Your **Imperial Majesty** once more that You might allow Your heart to be softened and not permit the loyal **Estates of the Empire** <term>Reichs Stände: the princes, prelates, and free cities that held a seat and a vote in the Imperial Diet</term> to be burdened and oppressed contrary to the **Imperial Constitutions** <term>Reichs-Constitutiones: the fundamental laws and legal framework of the Holy Roman Empire</term>, tradition, and freedom. Instead, may they at last be comforted, their grievances laudably and kindly remedied <note>original: remedirt</note>, the disputes among the Estates amicably settled, good trust established, and the beloved peace restored.

And yet, [I pray You] take it to heart as an Emperor where things might finally lead if the Estates should not be revived. I mean this uprightly, faithfully, and well, and I wish to remain Your **Imperial Majesty’s** and the Empire’s loyal **Prince-Elector** <term>Churfürst: one of the elite group of princes responsible for electing the Holy Roman Emperor</term>. ### No. 6.
## COPY.
### Handwritten letter from His Electoral Highness of Saxony
### to the King in Bohemia, dated Torgau,
### May 20, 1631.

Illustrious King, our willing and friendly service, and whatever more of love and goodness we are capable of, [are offered] to Your Royal Dignity beforehand. Friendly lo- <note>original: Liebe; the text breaks off here and will continue on the next page.</note>

<summary>Elector Johann Georg I of Saxony concludes his appeal to Emperor Ferdinand II for peaceful negotiations and begins a separate letter to the King in Bohemia. These documents reflect the high-stakes diplomacy of 1631 as Saxony navigated the complexities of the Thirty Years' War.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony, Torgau, Thirty Years' War, diplomacy, Estates of the Empire, King in Bohemia, Imperial Majesty</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter from Elector Johann Georg I of Saxony to the King in Bohemia (likely Frederick V, though he had been deposed, or perhaps referring to the Swedish King Gustavus Adolphus who was active in the region). The catchword "ber" from the previous page completes the salutation "Lieber" (Dear).</meta>

...dear <note>original: ber; completing the catchword "Lie-" from the previous page to form "Lieber"</note> Lord Uncle. Accordingly, the distinguished envoy of the Roman Imperial Majesty, our most gracious Lord—the steadfast, our dear and special Hans Ruprecht Hegenmüller of and at Dubenweiler on Albrechtsberg, His Imperial Majesty's Privy Councilor and Land Vice-Marshal <term>Landuntermarschall: a high-ranking administrative and judicial official in the Austrian territories</term> of the Archduchy of Austria below the Enns <note>original: Oesterreich unter der Ens; a historical name for Lower Austria</note>—has presented his mission to us. He has also delivered the friendly greeting <note>original: Salutation</note> entrusted to him by Your Royal Dignity, along with everything else pertaining to it.

Just as we abundantly perceive from this Your Royal Dignity’s great affection <note>original: affection</note> and friendly, well-inclined will toward us: so we justly offer our service-ready and friendly thanks for the same. We rejoice greatly that Your Royal Dignity is in good physical health and other royal prosperity. We wish that Divine Almightiness may not only sustain and preserve you further in such a state and in a long life, but also allow you to further prosper <note>original: prosperiren</note> in many ways to all success <note>original: feliciter</note>.

And because, following the proposal made by the aforementioned envoy of His Imperial Majesty, we have again most humbly declared ourselves in detail; and unfortunately, more than too clearly before our eyes <note>The text breaks off here; the Elector is likely beginning to describe the grim reality of the war visible "before his eyes."</note>

<summary>Elector Johann Georg I of Saxony receives an imperial envoy, Hans Ruprecht Hegenmüller, and responds to a friendly greeting from the King in Bohemia. The letter maintains the formal diplomatic protocols and well-wishes common among European nobility during the Thirty Years' War.</summary>
<keywords>Hans Ruprecht Hegenmüller, Lower Austria, diplomacy, Elector of Saxony, King in Bohemia, Thirty Years' War, 1631, statecraft</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues a diplomatic appeal from Elector Johann Georg I of Saxony. The previous page ended with a description of the dire situation visible "before our eyes." The syllable "gen" at the start of this page likely completes the verb "liegen" (to lie), as in "lie before our eyes."</meta>

...lie <note>original: gen; completing the verb "liegen" from the previous page to mean "lie before our eyes"</note> in what a highly sorrowful and extremely dangerous condition the beloved Fatherland of the German Nation <note>original: Vaterlandt Teutscher Nation</note> is caught. It is greatly to be feared that, unless it is quickly refreshed and comforted, and the burning flame of war <note>original: Kriegsflamm</note> is extinguished—through the restoration of upright German trust, the amicable settlement of pending disputes, and the rebuilding of a secure, general peace <note>original: Friedens</note> for which so many thousands of weeping and suffering people in bitter misery sigh and call unceasingly to God day and night—then everything will finally be cast into total conflagration <note>original: combustion; here meaning total destruction by fire or war</note> and ruin. Such a state of misery would then be introduced which could not be sufficiently lamented, and even less repaired.

Therefore, we request of Your Royal Dignity, out of our special and high trust, in a service-friendly manner: that you would, at a favorable opportunity, offer your high filial intercession and advocacy with the Roman Imperial Majesty <note>The Holy Roman Emperor, Ferdinand II</note>, your much-beloved Lord and Father. We ask that you help ensure our truly loyal concern and good intentions are most graciously accepted, that gentleness and kindness may prevail, and that the disputes which have persisted for many long years—and still remain—between the Catholic and Protestant Estates <term>Ständen: the organized social and political ranks of the Empire, such as princes and imperial cities, who held the right to participate in the Diet</term> may be settled in an amicable and Christian manner. Electors, Es- <note>original: Stä; catchword for the next page, likely "Ständen" (Estates)</note>

<summary>Elector Johann Georg I of Saxony describes the catastrophic state of Germany during the Thirty Years' War and begs the Emperor's son to intervene with his father to seek a peaceful settlement between religious factions.</summary>
<keywords>Thirty Years' War, Johann Georg I of Saxony, Holy Roman Empire, Peace of Prague, Catholic and Protestant Estates, diplomacy, German Fatherland</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the diplomatic correspondence of Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony, likely addressed to the King of Hungary and Bohemia (the future Emperor Ferdinand III). The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the need to settle disputes between the Catholic and Protestant Estates (the political ranks of the Empire).</meta>

[...and that the] Estates <note>original: Stände; the princes, counts, and imperial cities of the Holy Roman Empire</note> may be left with their rightful, so dearly acquired, and so firmly established freedoms; that the Fundamental Laws <note>original: Fundamental Gesetze; the core constitutional documents of the Empire</note> and Imperial Constitutions may remain in full force; that no one may be further burdened against them; and thus the weary and sinking Empire may be preserved from final ruin and saved from it. 

Through this, Your Royal Dignity shall achieve high and immortal praise and glory. The distressed Electors and Estates will be deeply obliged and bound to you, and you shall make yourself and your high Archducal House <note>The House of Habsburg, the ruling dynasty of the Holy Roman Empire</note> greatly beloved. We mean this—as we can testify with a loyal German heart—most sincerely and well, and we are at all times willing to perform friendly services for Your Royal Dignity. Dated Torgau, May 20, Anno 1631.

->Johann Georg, Elector, etc. <term>zc.: an abbreviation for "et cetera," used here to omit the Elector's lengthy list of formal titles.</term><-

### Extract from the Instructions which His Electoral Serenity of Saxony, etc., gave to his ambassadors sent to General Count Tilly <note>Johann Tserclaes, Count of Tilly, was the supreme commander of the Catholic League's forces during this phase of the Thirty Years' War.</note>, dated Leipzig, May 31, Anno 1631.

<summary>Elector Johann Georg I concludes an appeal for the preservation of imperial freedoms and begins a new set of instructions for his ambassadors to negotiate with the Catholic General Tilly.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony, Thirty Years' War, Imperial Constitutions, House of Habsburg, Count Tilly, Torgau, Leipzig, diplomacy, 1631</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the diplomatic instructions from Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony, to his ambassadors tasked with meeting General Tilly. It follows the heading "Extract from the Instructions..." established on the previous page. The catchword "bewil-" indicates the next page likely begins with a form of the word "bewilligen" (to grant or agree).</meta>

We sent him / the Lord General <note>Count Tilly, commander of the Catholic League's forces</note> / our most gracious greetings and wished him all the best; and it was made known to him how, a short time ago, the Roman Imperial Majesty / our most gracious Lord / had his Privy Councilor / Lord Hanns Ruprecht Hegemüller, etc., stay with us, and most graciously indicated that it was not against His Imperial Majesty's wishes that amicable negotiations <note>original: Tractaten; formal diplomatic discussions</note> be undertaken between himself and the Royal Dignity of Sweden <note>King Gustavus Adolphus</note>, etc. Furthermore, that His Majesty intended to entrust such matters to peace-loving Electors and Princes, but especially to Us, and that a cessation of arms <note>original: Anstand der Waffen; a temporary ceasefire</note> should be established for a considerable time. All of this he / the General / can see in more detail from the extract of the document of Imperial instructions presented by the envoy under letter A.

Now, regardless of all sorts of considerations <note>original: considerationen; obstacles or factors to weigh</note> lying in the way, we had thereupon most submissively declared that we would, to the best of our ability, take it upon ourselves to negotiate the amicable treaties and, for a certain time, a suspension of arms <note>original: suspensionem armorum</note> and truce. However, we first wished to communicate with him / the Lord General / regarding this and hear his views. Therefore, following this plan, we deemed it a necessity to dispatch you / our ambassadors / to him, to inquire how, in what manner, and under what conditions / with the Royal Dignity of Sweden, etc. / he / the Lord General / on behalf of His Imperial Majesty / [might proceed] toward the handling of peaceful negotiations, and then the establishment of a cessation of arms, to-

<summary>The Elector of Saxony informs General Tilly that the Emperor has authorized him to mediate a peace treaty and a ceasefire between the Empire and the King of Sweden. The Elector is sending ambassadors to Tilly to determine the specific conditions under which such a truce could be established.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Georg I, General Tilly, Hanns Ruprecht Hegemüller, Gustavus Adolphus, Sweden, diplomacy, ceasefire, Thirty Years' War, mediation</keywords>

[were] minded to grant / also how long a duration such a truce should be made for.

In addition to this, he should remind himself / of what he, the Lord General <note>Count Tilly, commander of the Catholic League forces</note>, sought from Us / namely, that the Protestant and Reforming Estates <note>original: Evangelische vnnd Protestierende Stände; the various territories and cities that had adopted the Reformation</note> should dismiss the troops they had raised for the rescue and protection of their lands and people / and that we should likewise obediently submit to the mandate of His Imperial Majesty <note>Ferdinand II</note> in this matter / along with further harsh conditions attached thereto.

Now, he himself should reasonably consider / whether the distressed state of affairs would be helped by such harsh procedures, never before seen in the Holy Roman Empire <term>H. Röm. Reich</term> / since the dearly sworn Laws of the Empire are clear / and it is only fair to uphold them / and rather look with laudable kindness upon how the suffering and exceedingly patient Estates might finally be truly relieved <note>original: exquisitet; released from their burdens</note> / not burdened against their freedoms and the Imperial Constitutions <term>Reichs-Constitutionen</term> / and so that / while one is trying to extinguish a fire / a larger one is not lit / and everything is utterly brought to combustion <note>total destruction by fire or war</note>. 

We also could not, for the Lord General’s further and consistent information on these matters, graciously hide from him / how the Elector of Mainz <note>original: Chur Mayntzens L.; referring to Anselm Casimir Wambold von Umstadt, the Arch-chancellor of the Empire</note> recently wrote to Us / and movingly / as can be seen from the attached Extract marked B / requested / that we might help prevent with diligent effort / the King of Sweden from further progress of war on the soil of the Empire / so that the hand of defense for His Imperial Majesty <term>Keys. Maj.</term> is not shortened / nor between the adherents of both religions <note>original: Religionsverwandten; meaning the Catholic and Protestant parties</note>

but they also further testified to their peace-loving inclination <note>original: "inclination"</note> toward a settlement and the hope we have conceived. To this end, Their Graces <note>original: "Ihrer L.", abbreviation for 'Ihre Liebden', a formal address between princes</note> have specifically sent word through two of their Privy Councilors, the Count von Trautmansdorff <note>Maximilian von Trautmansdorff (1584–1650), a prominent Imperial diplomat and peace negotiator</note> and the Imperial Vice-Chancellor Stralendorff <note>Peter Heinrich von Stralendorf (1580–1637)</note>. They reported in a friendly, cousinly, and filial manner that a splendid opening <note>original: "apertur"</note> now exists to attempt a friendly reconciliation <note>original: "Accommodation"</note> regarding the Swedish War, as well as to bring the grievances arising from the Edict <note>The Edict of Restitution (1629), a controversial decree by Emperor Ferdinand II that sought to reclaim Protestant lands for the Catholic Church</note> to an amicable settlement. As the Lord General <note>Likely Hans Georg von Arnim, the commander of the Saxon forces</note> may see in more detail from the original letters—which we have graciously shared with him for his review <note>original: "ad statum legendi"</note>—and likewise from what we have answered in good faith to the Elector of Mainz and the Lord Landgrave George of Hesse, as shown in copies C and D.

Since then, it can be understood in detail and at length from those documents—and especially from our most humble resolution <note>original: "Resolution"</note> delivered to His Imperial Majesty through his ambassadors, of which a copy is likewise attached under E—what the true status of all things is. This includes the true nature of the conclusion reached here at Leipzig <note>The Leipzig Manifesto of 1631, where Protestant princes declared their neutrality and organized a defensive army</note> and our current recruitment <note>original: "Werbung"</note> of troops. It serves no other purpose than that defense <note>original: "Defension"</note> permitted in Divine, Natural, international, and written laws, as well as the Imperial Constitutions, as we well [uphold]...

...could testify; considering that it is in accordance with the tradition in the Empire <note>original: "Herbringen im Reich", referring to the customary laws and established political practices of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, it was not intended to offend any person, much less to infringe in the slightest upon the high Imperial <term>authority</term> <note>original: "autoritæt"</note> of His Roman Imperial Majesty. Instead, it was <term>attested</term> <note>original: "contestiret", a formal legal declaration or protestation</note> that we intend to persist most submissively without interruption in the due obedience, loyalty, and <term>devotion</term> <note>original: "devotion", meaning political and personal fealty</note> owed to His Imperial Majesty. We have also proven ourselves laudably and steadfastly in this matter once again—both in our <term>Electoral</term> <note>original: "Churfürstlich", referring to his status as one of the prince-electors who chose the Emperor</note> offer made in the explanation to the Imperial envoy, and in our actions during the current dangerous state of affairs (which should rightly be highly praised and recognized).

Thus, We have the most gracious and certain confidence in him, the Lord General, that he will perceive our well-founded exposition, our most submissive and upright offer, and the attached most submissive petition as being legitimate, fair, peace-loving, upright, and faithfully intended. Furthermore, We believe that where the so dearly sworn laws, the law of nature, and the order of all nations apply, We should rightly not be ignored, much less threatened with force or burdened. This is especially true if our Electoral rank, dignity, and office are to be maintained, the traditions of the Empire are to remain active, and highly solemn promises are <term>put into effect</term> <note>original: "effectuirt"</note>. Moreover, good, useful, and brave service, as well as constant loyalty, should receive their glory and reward; even the honorable pagans <note>The Elector appeals to classical antiquity, suggesting that even non-Christians understood that breaking faith or failing to reward loyalty would invite divine vengeance</note> recognized this, judging that the gods would otherwise take revenge.

<meta>This page continues the diplomatic defense of Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony. ...constant love and loyalty which We bear toward His Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Keys. May."</note> were well known to the Lord General; he could therefore easily judge, according to his own brave spirit, how it must now displease, trouble, and pain Us—as a laudable <term>Elector</term> <note>Churfürst; one of the elite princes responsible for electing the Holy Roman Emperor</note> born of such an ancient house and so highly deserving—that they have proceeded against us in such a manner and even intended to threaten us with force. 

The Lord General himself may find, from copies of the extract marked "F," that even now the Electoral Lord of Mainz <note>The Archbishop-Elector of Mainz, the highest-ranking prince in the Empire</note> attests how little the Catholic side was pleased by the recent military marches, <term>contributions</term> <note>forced taxes or supplies demanded by occupying armies</note>, and other excesses. He also attests that in order to preserve the Fundamental Laws of the Empire <note>original: "FundamentalReichsGesetze"</note>, as well as the universally beneficial constitution and ordinances, the highly valued <term>Religious and Secular Peace</term> <note>original: "Religion- vnd ProphanFrieden"; referring to the Peace of Augsburg (1555) and the public peace of the Empire</note>, and the liberty and dignity of the <term>Estates</term> <note>the various princes, lords, and cities that held a seat in the Imperial Diet</note>, neither diligence, effort, nor expense has been spared. 

What specifically the Electoral Lord of Bavaria <note>The leader of the Catholic League</note> laudably put forward in public session <note>original: "in publico Consessu"</note> at the most recent assembly held in Regensburg—to his great and high fame—regarding this point, and the fact that the laws could not be overstepped in this matter due to any necessity, was known to him, the Lord General himself; and in this regard, We refer to the <term>protocols</term> <note>original: "Protocolla"; the official minutes of the Imperial proceedings</note>.

The point regarding the so dearly acquired and highly secured freedom, and that the fundamental laws and Imperial constitutions be maintained in their <term>vigor</term> <note>original: "vigor"; meaning their legal force and effectiveness</note>, concerns all Estates of the Empire collectively, without distinction of religion. Therefore, we cannot do anything to their <term>prejudice</term> <note>original: "præjudiciren"; to act in a way that harms or diminishes their legal rights</note>. ...as a movable pillar of the same <note>The Holy Roman Empire</note>, and accordingly bound by office, status, and duty to uphold the honor and welfare of the Realm. The Lord General should further consider that if the <term>Protestant Estates</term> <note>Protestirend: Stände; the Lutheran and Reformed territories and princes within the Empire</note> should be further burdened, or otherwise be overrun and oppressed with force, minds would be even more wounded <note>original Latin: "vulneriret"</note> and greater divisions <note>original Latin: "distractiones"</note> caused. This would not promote the upcoming peaceful <term>negotiations</term> <note>original: "Tractaten"</note>, but would rather take on a strange and questionable appearance—as if one were proposing peaceful dealings and even setting a date for an assembly <note>Tagesarth; a formal day appointed for a diplomatic meeting or Diet</note>, declaring oneself inclined toward all peaceability, while in the meantime <note>original Latin: "interim"</note> intending to overrun the Estates with military power and place them in even greater misery and compulsion.

His Grace the <term>Elector of Mainz</term> <note>Chur Mäyntzen; the Archbishop-Elector of Mainz, often the mediator of the Imperial Diets</note> himself sought that, in the period between now and then, no hostilities should be stirred up between the adherents of either religion, and the goal of the peace process should not be disrupted. He also pledged that his side would neglect nothing to achieve a similar effect. We also could not see how these things could stand together: to <term>negotiate</term> <note>original: "tractiren"</note> peacefully, yet still terrify the Estates with military force. And what the Catholic Lords in their high positions sought—namely, that all hostilities should cease—that would indeed be, according to Holy Scripture, all laws, and the teachings of reason, especially fair, so that it should not be denied to the other side either.

We declare Ourselves once again to the effect that We are and shall remain the loyal and obedient <term>Elector</term> <note>Churfürst</note> of the Roman Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Röm. Keys. Ma."</note>, and also toward our fellow Electors, by virtue of the sworn <note>catchword: "geschwo" (geschworenen)</note>

...spirit in which we have otherwise at all times sensed his mind <note>original: "Gemüth"</note> inclined toward peace and tranquility. He has offered this once again in the letter written to us on the 14/24 <note>This dual date represents the Julian and Gregorian calendars, which were both in use during this period</note> of this month. According to his influential standing <term>valor</term> <note>original: "valor"; referring to the weight or value of his influence</note>, he shall do his best service in this matter, promote the adoption of peaceful means, and not proceed against the **Evangelical and Protestant Estates** with any military force or the burdens of war. Much less should he allow himself to be moved to undertake anything hostile, as common rumor suggests. For he can reasonably judge that this is not the way to restore peace and tranquility in the **Empire** <note>original: "Imperio"</note> at all; rather, it would likely cause even greater disaster, and perhaps even **despair** <note>original: "desperation"</note>, and create a lamentable and highly damaging rift and **rupture** <note>original: "ruptur"</note> in the Empire.

Instead, in place of rescue, they would be overwhelmed with force and distressed. Almighty God is a God of justice, indeed justice itself, and He wishes to see the oaths and promises kept by both one side and the other. We mean this quite faithfully and well, seeking nothing other than peace and tranquility, and we intend, as befits an **Elector** <note>Churfürst; one of the elite princes of the Holy Roman Empire responsible for electing the Emperor</note>, to behave accordingly in the future. Thus, we also hold toward Your **Imperial Majesty** the most humble and certain confidence that You will be most graciously and well satisfied with our most humble explanation, delivered to Your ambassadors.

# COPY
### Of the handwritten letter from His Electoral Highness of Saxony etc. to the Lord Elector of Mainz / dated Leipzig, May 24, the year 1631.

My friendly service and whatever else I may offer of love and goodness first, **Most Reverend** <note>The Elector of Mainz was an Archbishop and the Archchancellor of the Empire, holding the highest-ranking seat among the Electors</note>, especially dear Friend.

**Your Love's** <note>original: "Ewer L."/ "Liebden." A standard honorific used between German princes of equal rank to denote cousinly affection and mutual respect</note> handwritten letter has reached me safely, and I have learned from it that Your Love duly received, opened, read, and forwarded to the aforementioned fellow **Electors** <note>The high-ranking princes who held the right to elect the Holy Roman Emperor</note> the writing dispatched by all the Electors and **Estates** <note>The various territories and rulers represented in the Imperial Diet</note> recently assembled at **Leipzig** <note>A reference to the Leipzig Convention of February-April 1631, where Protestant princes met to discuss a third path between the Emperor and the King of Sweden</note> to Your [Love] and your Catholic fellow Electors.

I also understand that these fellow Electors subsequently arranged a **federal assembly** <note>original: "Bundestag"</note> at **Dinkelsbühl** <note>A city in Bavaria</note>, and were for that reason still gathered there. Furthermore, I see that the main response is hopefully expected to follow without delay. In the meantime, however, you wished to provide me with a preliminary report on where matters stand, to further disclose your state of mind at length, and to communicate that all this flows from the upright trust which has **continued** <note>original: "continuant"</note> for many long years between Your Love’s ancestors and my own praiseworthy forebears, to the notable benefit and advantage of the entire **Holy Roman Empire** <note>original: "Römischen Reich"</note>.

<summary>Johann Georg I of Saxony writes to the Elector of Mainz, acknowledging that the Catholic Estates are meeting in Dinkelsbühl to discuss the grievances raised by the Protestant princes at the Leipzig Convention. On the contrary, it is much more greatly to be feared that if one continues to proceed only with force, and if no prayer or pleading—

<meta>This page continues the defense by Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony, regarding the "Leipzig Manifesto" (February-April 1631). He is justifying why the Protestant princes formed a defensive alliance, arguing it was a legal necessity for self-preservation rather than a rebellion against the Emperor.</meta>

...no right nor law could help any longer, and it should serve to provide comfort and support to those most extremely oppressed and tormented, so that finally a desperate act might follow. For what misery and destruction such overly harsh actions have often caused within governments can be read in the history books, and is partly still visible before our eyes.

Never could I have presumed, nor should I have, that the **Defensive Constitution** <note>original: "Defensions Verfassung" / Refers to the agreement made at the Leipzig Convention to raise an army for the defense of Protestant territories.</note> agreed upon at **Leipzig**—though intended within certain limits against the violence forbidden in the laws and **Imperial Constitutions** <term>Reichs-Constitutionen: The fundamental laws and treaties governing the Holy Roman Empire.</term>—would be taken so poorly and interpreted in such a manner. 

Because such an agreement is:
1. In accordance with Divine, Natural, and International law, as well as written laws; 
2. Permitted in the **Imperial Constitutions**; 
3. And even in the year 1623 <note>original: "Anno 1623"</note> specifically, and indeed upon this very foundation: that it is the duty of **Electors** <term>Chur-Fürsten: The high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who elected the Emperor.</term> and **Estates** to take their subjects and the lands and people entrusted to them into proper care and obligatory protection, so that they might be saved from total ruin, and so that what they still have and possess might not be seized through further dangerous military marches and **quartering** <note>original: "Einlagerung" / The forced housing and feeding of soldiers by civilians, which often led to the economic exhaustion of a region.</note>, and they be brought to utter desperation. 

Thus it was held, and was also most graciously regarded by **His Imperial Majesty** <term>Keys. Mayst. (Kaiserliche Majestät): The Emperor, Ferdinand II.</term> as a peace-loving demonstration, as has been detailed more extensively in my most humble response to **His Imperial Majesty**. I am aware of no *new alliance* <note>original: "Newen Bündnuß"</note>, nor shall I be convinced of one; and I can demonstrate through the records and my resolutions contained therein that I do not agree to such a thing, but rather 

<summary>Johann Georg I defends the Leipzig Defensive Constitution as a legal and moral necessity to protect subjects from the ruinous effects of military quartering and illegal violence. He argues that such actions are consistent with Imperial law and were previously accepted by the Emperor in 1623.</summary>

<keywords>Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony, Leipzig Convention, Defensive Constitution, Imperial Constitutions, military quartering, self-defense, Holy Roman Empire, Protestant Estates</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal and political justification for the Protestant alliance formed at the Leipzig Convention in 1631. The author, Elector Johann Georg I of Saxony, argues that the right to self-defense is a natural right and consistent with the laws of the Holy Roman Empire, citing historical precedents from the Catholic side to support his position.</meta>

<sig>D ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: tzes Regi.</meta>

...intend to remain within the **Imperial Constitutions**. The **Leipzig Manifesto** <note>original: "Leipzigische Abschied" / The formal resolution of the Protestant estates meeting in Leipzig.</note> is founded and based upon the general **Imperial Recess** <term>Reichs-Recess: The formal collection of laws passed by the Imperial Diet, here specifically referring to the 1555 Peace of Augsburg.</term> established in the year 1555, which everyone is anyway obliged to follow. Therefore, I still cannot understand why the **Law of Nature** <note>original: "Recht der Natur" / In this context, the fundamental right to self-preservation and defense against illegal force.</note> should be forbidden to the **Evangelical** and **Protesting Estates**, or why their **Defensive Constitution** should be considered unjustifiable. This is especially true since it was explicitly agreed therein that no one should be offended by it, and it should remain entirely within the boundaries of the **Imperial Constitutions**. Furthermore, it is clearly **asserted** <term>contestirt: From the Latin 'contestari,' meaning to formally declare or bear witness.</term> and testified therein that they will continue to persist in the most humble and uninterrupted obedience, loyalty, and **devotion** <term>Devotion: A formal expression of submission and service to the Emperor.</term> belonging to His **Roman Imperial Majesty**.

When the **United** <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant princes formed in 1608.</note> in **the month of December in the year 1619** <note>original: "Mense Decembri Anno 1619."</note> addressed the embassy of His Lordship the **Elector of Bavaria** <note>Maximilian I, the leader of the Catholic League.</note> and complained loudly about the military arrangement that His Lordship and the **Bishop of Würzburg** were making, the ambassadors answered this point as follows: That His Lordship, referring to the Bishop of Würzburg, recognized an obligation—in order to avert such and other distress, sudden attacks, and unforeseen burdens—to consider a necessary **defense** <note>original: "Defension"</note> in a permitted manner and at the right time. This would not be according to another’s judgment, but their own, as they knew best what and how much was necessary for it. Likewise (which is well worth noting), they further stated in such a dispatch: Much less has any **Catholic** [Estate] to this date [raised] one or more entire **regiments**...

<summary>Johann Georg I defends the Protestant defensive alliance by arguing it is consistent with the Peace of Augsburg (1555) and the Law of Nature, while citing a 1619 precedent where Catholic princes used the same justification for their own military preparations.</summary>

...carried out the government, or acted against the **Imperial Constitution** and **Circle Ordinance** <term>Circle Ordinance: The "Kreyß Ordnung" governed the administrative districts (Circles) of the Holy Roman Empire, overseeing local peace and defense.</term>, because the recruitment of troops, in accordance with the **Imperial Constitutions**, can no more be forbidden to them for their own **defense** <note>original: "Defension"</note> than it can be to others. This is an argument that the **Evangelical** and **Protesting Estates** <note>The Protestant princes and territories.</note> may now also justly offer and cite as a precedent.

For if the **Catholic League**, and the constitution that followed it, were not contrary to the **Imperial Constitutions** and **Circle Ordinances**—as was asserted in that official dispatch—then now, *while the balance of law and justice remains equal* <note>original: "stante iuris & iustitiæ equilibrio"</note>, the necessary **defensive constitution** of the **Evangelical and Protesting Estates** cannot and should not be misinterpreted. This is especially true since they have not established any new league among themselves, but rather *adhere to* <note>original: "inhærit"</note> the existing **Imperial Constitutions** and **Circle Ordinances**.

What else must be considered regarding alliances—what harmful mistrust they cause, and what calamity and *division* <note>original: "Distraction"</note> they are accustomed to introducing into the **Commonwealth**—is well known to me, praise be to God. I have always diligently guarded myself against them. Likewise, when the **Protestant Union** emerged in the Empire and the **Catholic counter-league** followed it, I earnestly and faithfully advised against them. I constantly reminded everyone with the greatest diligence that we should be content with the general *Imperial bonds* <note>original: "Reichsvinculis" / The legal and constitutional ties that already connect the members of the Empire.</note>, through which everyone is already sufficiently *obligated* <note>original: "obligiret"</note> and bound to one another. Had my faithful and well-meaning advice been followed, the state of the **Holy Roman Empire** would certainly be much better than it unfortunately appears before our eyes today.

The suggestion made regarding foreign **Potentates** <note>original: "Potentaten" / Foreign rulers or sovereign powers, likely referring to the King of Sweden or the King of France.</note>...

<summary>Elector Johann Georg I argues that the Protestant alliance is legal by comparing it to the earlier Catholic League, asserting that if one was constitutional, the other must be as well. I bear witness to my loyalty and sincerity; and finally, if no amount of begging, pleading, or humble and peaceful offerings are heard, but instead violence is to be the law, I will be blameless before GOD and all the world for any calamity that follows. Your Graciousness may consider everything most rationally for yourself, and where it might lead in such a case.

I wished to report this to you in a friendly reply, and am always willing and diligent to perform pleasant services for Your Graciousness. Given at **Torgau** on the 24th of May, in the year 1631.

->John George / **Elector**, etc.<-

I have indeed done so loyally; yet now, as stated above, I am to receive this "reward" <note>The Elector is speaking sarcastically here; his "reward" for loyalty is being treated with suspicion or threatened with military force.</note> through no fault of my own.

I remember well the ancient and great friendship that has been handed down between our praiseworthy ancestors on both sides, and how they always concerned themselves with the high benefit and welfare of the Holy Roman Empire. ### No. 11.
## COPY
### Of the Electoral Highness of Saxony’s etc. Letter of Protection regarding the Leipzig Resolution, to the Lord Elector of Mainz etc. on the 10th of July, the year 1631.

Our friendly service and whatever we may offer in much love and goodness first, **Most Reverend** <note>This title acknowledges that the Elector of Mainz was also an Archbishop and the Archchancellor of the Empire.</note>, especially dear friend.

Regarding what Your Graciousness and the others of our Lords, the **Catholic** fellow **Electors** <term>Churfürsten<gloss>The elite group of princes—originally seven—who held the exclusive right to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</gloss></term> [have heard] from the **Protestant** <note>original: *Evangelischen vnd Protestirenden*</note> Electors, Princes, and **Estates** <term>Stände<gloss>The territories and social ranks that had a seat and a vote in the Imperial Diet (Reichstag).</gloss></term> at Leipzig, on account of the highly burdensome and unbearable tribulations of war; and furthermore, that the disputes which have existed for many long years and still [persist] between the Catholic and Protestant Estates

We are not a little saddened by the unfortunate times into which we have recently fallen. However, if kindness had been preferred over severity—as we have often faithfully and movingly reminded others—and if new complications had not been caused in almost every corner with foreign potentates (even though the **Golden Bull** <term>Güldene Bull<gloss>The foundational constitutional document of the Holy Roman Empire (1356), which regulated the election of the Emperor and the rights of the Electors.</gloss></term> as well as the **Royal Capitulation** <term>Königliche Capitulation<gloss>The *Wahlkapitulation*, a written agreement or contract that a candidate for the throne had to sign before being elected Emperor, limiting their power and protecting the rights of the Estates.</gloss></term> expressly state that good friendship should be maintained with such powers), and this without the advice and consent of the **Empire** <term>Reich<gloss>The Holy Roman Empire.</gloss></term>, and if the soldiery, which...

[...previously, for] a long time, [those who] have burdened the exceedingly patient, obedient, and loyal **Imperial Estates** <term>Reichsstände<gloss>The territories and authorities within the Holy Roman Empire that held a seat and a vote in the Imperial Diet.</gloss></term> / against the **Imperial Constitutions** <term>Reichsconstitutiones<gloss>The fundamental laws and legal framework of the Holy Roman Empire.</gloss></term> and their rightful, dearly and bravely won freedoms / [those who] have miserably devastated their lands / and behaved wickedly within them / and have not been sent away to various foreign places / [if only] one had also let the beneficial, sworn **Imperial laws** remain the guiding principle for all **actions** <note>original: *Actionen*</note> in every respect—and indeed for one side as much as the other / and had not become so arrogant through shifting fortunes and great military power / and now resolved to push through with it / then things would, if God wills, stand better in our beloved Fatherland of the **German Nation**.

<meta>This page continues the legal argument regarding the rights of the Estates of the Holy Roman Empire. Furthermore, Your Graces must acknowledge along with us that in the so highly-vouchsafed, established, and in the year 1548 renewed and confirmed **Public Peace** <term>LandFriede <gloss>The *Landfrieden* was a fundamental law of the Holy Roman Empire designed to end private feuds and ensure that legal disputes were settled in court rather than on the battlefield.</gloss></term>—to which His Imperial Majesty himself, as Roman Emperor, on behalf of the **Empire** and his hereditary lands, and all the Estates of the **Empire**, have bound themselves **by way of a contract** <note>original: "per modum contractus"</note>—it is provided and contained in these bright, clear words: That from the time of this proclamation, no one, of whatever dignity, status, or nature they may be, for no cause whatsoever, by whatever name it may be called, or under whatever sought-after pretext it may occur, shall feud against, war with, rob, capture, attack, or besiege another <term>etc.</term> 

For this reason, our grandfather, Elector Augustus of Saxony of highly praiseworthy memory, at the assembly at Rothenburg on the Tauber in the year 1584, through his councilors—according to the instructions granted to them—had this poignantly recalled in these words: That His Imperial Majesty must by all means allow the Electors, Princes, and Estates—and in turn, the Estates toward His Imperial Majesty, as well as one Estate toward another—to remain in all the contents of the *Religious* <note>This refers to the Peace of Augsburg (1555), which granted legal recognition to Lutheranism within the Empire.</note> and general *Constitution* of the established **Public Peace**. 

His Imperial Majesty has also bound himself to this **expressly** <note>original: "per expressum"</note> in his royal, highly-solemnly sworn **Capitulation** <term>Capitulation <gloss>The *Wahlkapitulation* (Electoral Capitulation) was a mandatory contract signed by a candidate for the Imperial throne before his election, ensuring he would respect the traditional rights and liberties of the German princes.</gloss></term>, and moreover, in Section 25 of the aforementioned *Capitulation*, promised with these words: We shall and will also the Electors, Princes, Prelates...

<meta>This page continues the legal argument asserting that the military actions of the Catholic League and Imperial forces violate established Imperial law. It transitions from discussing the "Defensionsverfassung" (Defense Constitution) to citing specific treaties and the Emperor's own sworn oaths.</meta>

Furthermore, Your Graces must also acknowledge along with us that in the highly valued, universally established, and—in the *year* 1548—renewed and confirmed **Public Peace** <term>LandFriede <gloss>The Perpetual Public Peace (Ewiger Landfriede) was a legal treaty within the Holy Roman Empire designed to end private feuds and ensure internal security through the rule of law rather than force.</gloss></term>, to which His Imperial Majesty himself, as Roman Emperor, on behalf of the **Empire** and his hereditary lands, and all the Estates of the Empire, have bound themselves *by way of contract* <note>original: "per modum contractus"</note>, it is provided and contained in these bright, clear words: That from the time of this proclamation, no one, of whatever dignity, status, or being they may be, for any reason whatsoever, whatever it may be named, or under whatever sought appearance it may happen, shall feud with, war against, rob, seize, invade, or besiege the other, *etc.* 

Therefore, our grandfather <note>original: "Großherr Vater"; this likely refers to Elector Augustus of Saxony (reigned 1553–1586), an ancestor of the author.</note>, Elector *Augustus* of Saxony, of highly praised memory, at the assembly at Rottenburg an der Tauber in the *year* 1584, through his councilors—according to the *instruction* given to them—vividly reminded everyone with these words: That His Imperial Majesty shall in every way leave the Electors, Princes, and Estates—and in turn, the Estates shall leave His Imperial Majesty, as well as one Estate the other—in peace regarding the <term>Religious Peace <gloss>Refers to the Peace of Augsburg (1555), which allowed princes to choose either Lutheranism or Catholicism as the official religion of their territory.</gloss></term> and the general <term>Constitution <gloss>The fundamental legal framework and set of imperial laws governing the Holy Roman Empire.</gloss></term> of the established **Public Peace**, in all its content. 

His Imperial Majesty has also bound himself *expressly* <note>original: "per expressum"</note> to this in his royal, highly and dearly sworn <term>Capitulation <gloss>The Wahlkapitulation was a contract a candidate for Holy Roman Emperor was forced to sign before his election, guaranteeing the rights and privileges of the Imperial Estates.</gloss></term>. Furthermore, in Section 25 of the mentioned <term>Capitulation</term>, he promised with these words: We shall and will also [leave] the Electors, Princes, Prelates,

### COPY
### Of the letter from the Count of Fürstenberg to His Electoral Serenity of Saxony, etc.
### Dated Ilmenau, August 17,
### Anno 1631.

Most Serene Elector <note>The title "Durchlaucht" (Serenity) was a high-ranking style of address reserved for the Electors who had the power to choose the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>, Most Gracious Lord, my most humble and obedient services are at all times at the disposal of Your Electoral Serenity.

Your Electoral Serenity will, without doubt and without my reminder, have been graciously informed that I have been most graciously commanded by His Roman Imperial Majesty <term>Römische Kaiserliche Majestät <gloss>The formal title for the Holy Roman Emperor, at this time Ferdinand II of the House of Habsburg.</gloss></term>, my most gracious Lord, to join <note>original: "conjungeiren," a Germanization of the Latin *conjungere*, meaning to unite or link military forces</note> with General Tilly <note>Johann Tserclaes, Count of Tilly, was the supreme commander of the Catholic League's forces and a key figure in the Imperial military hierarchy.</note> and the army under my command.

Although I would have wished for nothing more in this undertaking than to have been able to spare Your Electoral Serenity’s lands from my planned march, I have nevertheless been unavoidably compelled to traverse <note>original: "bereten," likely meaning to ride through or temporarily occupy</note> the County of Henneberg for a short time. There, I was supplied with the necessary provisions according to my needs by the Chief Overseers at Schleusingen. For this, I not only offer my most obedient thanks to Your Electoral Serenity, but

For this, I not only offer my most obedient thanks to Your Electoral Serenity, but

I shall have maintained such good order <note>In this context, "order" refers to military discipline, ensuring that his troops do not pillage or mistreat the local population while passing through.</note> therein that no one shall have cause to bring any complaint against me.

I most obediently request Your Electoral Serenity to remain assured at all times of my due devotion to you, and that I am and remain as eager and willing as possible to perform all most obedient services for you at all times.

I commend Your Electoral Serenity to the dear Almighty God for constant physical health and all your own desired happy prosperity, while most humbly commending myself to your persistent Electoral grace. Done in the Headquarters at Ilmenau <note>A town in Thuringia; its location was strategically significant for troop movements during the Thirty Years' War.</note>, the 17th of August, in the year 1631.

### N. 12.
### COPY
### Letter from the Count of Fürstenberg to His Electoral Serenity of Saxony etc.
### dated Ilmenau, 17th August
### In the Year 1631.

Most Serene Elector, Most Gracious Lord, my most humble and obedient services are at Your Electoral Serenity's disposal at all times.

Your Electoral Serenity will, without any reminder from me, undoubtedly have been graciously informed that I have been most graciously commanded by His Roman Imperial Majesty <note>Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II.</note>, my most gracious Lord, to join myself and my subordinate army with Lord General Tilly <note>Jean Tserclaes, Count of Tilly, the supreme commander of the Imperial forces who was at this time pressuring Saxony to abandon its neutrality.</note>.

Although I wished for nothing more in this undertaking than to have been able to spare Your Electoral Serenity's lands from my planned march, I have nevertheless been unavoidably compelled to traverse the County of Henneberg <note>A territory in Thuringia; after the Henneberg line died out, it was administered by the various branches of the Saxon House of Wettin.</note> for a short time. While there, I was supplied with the necessary provisions as required by the High Overseer at Schleusingen <note>A town that served as the administrative center for the County of Henneberg.</note>. For this, I not only offer my most obedient thanks to Your Electoral Serenity, but—

<summary>This page begins a formal letter from the Imperial General Count von Fürstenberg to the Elector of Saxony, explaining that his army is marching through Saxon-administered territory to link up with General Tilly's forces. It strikes a polite but firm diplomatic tone, acknowledging the use of local resources while asserting Imperial military necessity.</summary>

and brought forward / and also handed over in writing by the same / His aforementioned Electoral Serenity has listened to and heard this with due *reverence* / and has diligently read and maturely considered the *communicated* document / which is a *copy* of the *instruction* given to the Lord *Sub-delegates* <note>original: *Subdelegirte*; envoys acting on behalf of a higher authority, here Count Tilly</note> by His *Excellence* / and could find nothing else therein / than that it is lengthily cited and recounted in *substance* / why His Imperial Majesty still bears such high *displeasure* <note>original: *displicentz*</note> and dissatisfaction regarding the **Leipzig Resolution** <note>The *Leipziger Schluss* of 1631 was an agreement among Protestant princes to raise their own armies to defend their lands, which the Emperor viewed as an act of rebellion.</note> / Therefore, His Electoral Serenity is most graciously requested / not only to personally desist from the intended recruitment <note>original: *Werbung*; the raising of new military forces</note> / and to leave his people <note>his soldiers</note> on all sides to His Imperial Majesty along with the associated *contributions* <note>original: *Contributionibus*; military taxes or supplies levied on a population</note> / but also to restrain his fellow Evangelical and Protestant Electors, Princes, and Estates from all complications / to observe the Imperial *mandates* <note>official decrees</note> / and not to let everything come *to extremes* <note>original: *ad extrema*; the point of total war or irreversible crisis</note>.

As for what concerns the main matter itself / His Electoral Serenity <note>John George I of Saxony</note> considers it—out of most humble, high reverence and the duty of respect he faithfully bears toward His Imperial Majesty <note>Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II</note> as his most honored and beloved sovereign—to be unhelpful at this time to enter into a long dispute regarding the point <note>original: *super puncto*</note> of the Leipzig Conclusion <note>The "Leipzig Manifesto" of 1631, where Protestant princes organized for self-defense while claiming to remain loyal to the Emperor</note>; nor would the matter be helped by doing so / but instead [he] refers and retreats briefly to his previous most humble Justification Writing <note>A formal legal document justifying his political and military actions</note> regarding this step <note>original: *in hoc passu*</note> / in which he has already most humbly and openly revealed to His Imperial Majesty / what has compelled His Electoral Serenity to form the Defensive Arrangement <note>term: <term>Defensions Verfassung</term> — the military league established by the Protestant estates for mutual protection</note>—which is well-founded in Divine, Natural, International <note>literally "the rights of all peoples"</note>, and Secular Law / but especially also in the Constitutions of the Holy Empire and established custom—and what his sole intentions were in doing so. But so that Your Excellence and everyone / might more clearly see how the justice of the aforementioned compelled Defensive Arrangement stands / and how it is grounded according to the traditions of the Empire / whether one looks at the ancient <note>original: *antiqua*</note> or the newest accepted examples <note>original: *novissima recepta Exempla*</note> / His Electoral Serenity hereby communicates to the same / what he wrote in well-meaning confidence a short time ago to his beloved fellow-Elector of Mainz <note>Anselm Casimir Wambold von Umstadt, a powerful Catholic Prince-Bishop and Archchancellor of the Empire</note> etc. / according to established custom. And finally, His Electoral Serenity can leave the judgment <note>original: *Dijudication*</note> and recognition of this matter to the whole honorable world / and hereby reserves the right to provide further modest and most humble explanations as the necessity arises. And

not burden [them] with **billeting** <note>original: *Einquartirungen* — the forced housing of soldiers in civilian homes</note> / **passage of troops** / and other **hardships of war** / but rather let them be completely spared. For His Electoral Serenity <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony</note> himself / as touched upon with high reason in the petition / finds / that through such unmerited hostilities <note>original: *hostiliteten*</note> the matter is not helped / but rather spirits would be more and more **exacerbated** <note>original: *exacerbiert* — from Latin *exacerbare*, to irritate or provoke</note> and embittered / And it could also easily be calculated here / where it might finally lead / if one were to **proceed** <note>original: *procedirt*</note> so hostilely.

Which His Electoral Serenity wished to report to the Lord **Deputies** <note>original: *Subdelegirten*</note> / for their **resolution** <note>original: *Resolution*</note> / and [he] remains well-disposed toward them with Electoral grace and goodness. **Signed** <note>original: *Signatum*</note> at Merseburg on the 21st of **August, in the year** 1631.

->N. 14.<-
### COPY
<note>original: *COPIA*</note>
### Of His Electoral Serenity's latest letter 
### written to General
### Tilly / **dated** Tor-
<note>Johann Tserclaes, Count of Tilly (1559–1632), was the supreme commander of the Catholic League forces during the Thirty Years' War.</note>
->gaw the 3rd of **September, in the year** 1631.<-

Our greetings first / Well-born, dear and special [friend] / We have well received your various letters / concerning the **resolution** / which We recently granted to your **deputies** / upon their petition and the copy of the **instructions** <note>original: *Instruction*</note> presented to us / and from this / besides the expressed thanks for the **audience** <note>original: *audientz*</note> granted to your said deputies / [we] have understood among other things / as if His Roman Imperial Majesty, Our most gracious Lord / would not be satisfied with Our **contestations** <note>original: *Contestationibus* — formal legal protests or declarations</note>—

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the Elector of Saxony’s formal response to General Tilly. The text picks up from the previous page's catchword "vngnü-" (enough/satisfied), completing the phrase to show the Elector’s stance on the Imperial demands.</meta>

be satisfied. For this reason, you seek once more by citing all sorts of motives to ensure that we do not allow ourselves to be led into any **desperate measures** <note>original: *desperata Consilia* — Latin for "desperate counsels," referring to extreme or rash political actions</note> / but rather that we should, through most obedient **compliance** <note>original: *parition* — from Latin *paritio*, the act of obeying a legal summons or command</note>, carry out without refusal that which the **Imperial Mandates of Revocation** <note>original: *Keys. Avocatorial-Mandata* — high-level legal decrees by the Emperor commanding subjects to cease certain actions or return to loyalty</note> contained / and which was most graciously intended through the **instruction** <note>original: *Instruction*</note> handed to us by your **deputies** <note>original: *subdelegirten*</note>. For in the unexpected adverse case of non-compliance, you would conversely be compelled to undertake and execute what was in accordance with the command of His Imperial Majesty, our most gracious Lord / and what the unavoidable necessity and **nature of war** <note>original: *Kriegs-beschaffenheit*</note> unalterably required.

Now, at the outset, there was no need at all for the thanks given / for our duty toward His aforementioned Imperial Majesty required such / we would also have wished to answer your aforementioned letters on the main point long ago / had we not remained in good confidence that the robbing, plundering, and burning in our lands would have ceased / since you report that you have forbidden such / and that the places taken from us by force would have been fully restored. Therefore, you will not find this delay unjust.

Regarding the main matter / we hope to have explained ourselves most humbly and laudably in the **resolution** given in writing to the Lord Deputies / in such a way as our bounden obedience, the **Imperial Constitutions** <note>original: *Reichs-Constitutiones* — the fundamental laws and treaties that governed the Holy Roman Empire</note>, tradition, and the state of affairs demand / we had also lived in the good hope that His Imperial Majesty would require nothing more from us than what we owe according to the sworn **Imperial Laws** <note>original: *Reichs-Gesetze*</note> / and therefore would have been most graciously satisfied with our most obedient offer. We must finally leave the **judgment** <note>original: *adjudication*</note> of this to those at home and abroad, indeed to the whole honorable world.

What sort of obedience we are bound to perform for His Roman Imperial Majesty / as a faithful **Prince-Elector** <note>original: *Churfürst* — one of the elite group of princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor; Saxony was one of the most powerful Protestant electorates</note> of the Empire / is known to us, praise be to GOD / and we have [shown] it in our electoral reign, now guided by God's goodness for twenty years, un-
